#7353. Future of TV Briefing: What it costs brands to fund long-form creator series

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Future of TV Briefing covers the latest in streaming and TV for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Wednesday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

This week’s Future of TV Briefing looks at the economics behind brands sponsoring creator-led long-form original video series.

A creator-led long-form original video series can cost a brand anywhere from $150,000 to $5 million, according to Cyndi Steele, a former TV executive producer who leads talent management firm G&B Digital Management’s &Beyond Creators’ Studio.

A six- to seven-figure investment may be an eye-popping amount for CFOs wary of buying into creators. But paying that premium begins to make more sense as video platforms like YouTube, Instagram and TikTok have started highlighting serialized shows and as brands treat creators less like media properties and more like creative collaborators.

Fifty-seven percent of brands said that generating content is a goal for their creator campaigns, according to a study by LTK and Northwestern University released last month. Your reaction to that stat may be something like, “Isn’t generating content 100% the point of brands hiring creators for campaigns?” Yeah. But the shift underlying the stat is how brands are looking to squeeze more content out of the content that creators produce beyond social.

Recycling creator content to this extent requires a lot of content. Which is where the long-form series pitch can come in.

Consider a brand-sponsored, creator-led series that spans 10 long-form episodes plus three short-form videos per episode for a total of 40 pieces of content. That can average between $300,000 to $500,000, according to Steele. Again, that’s a pretty penny. But consider the cost for a brand to produce a traditional TV show.

“When I was in unscripted reality TV, let’s say 10 episodes of a 40-minute-long show, the budget for that is usually around like $5 million. That’s only to do literally the physical production, nothing else,” Steele said.

There are different levers a brand can pull on to adjust the costs of a creator-led long-form show. The episode count can be changed, for example. But the primary line items include:

IP ownership is the least settled aspect of the deal, as Digiday recently reported. For starters, it’s subject to how many ownership the creator retains as well as &Beyond’s ownership stake. The studio prefers first to agree to an ownership split with the creator.

“If it’s an original concept that we’re developing as the exclusive development and production partner, we do split ownership with our talent. A lot of times we like to have the talent typically have the majority ownership. So it a lot of times looks like a 60-40 or 70-30 [split],” Steele said.

But then where does the brand’s cut come from? Well, that becomes a re-appraisal of everyone’s equity. But only if the brand opts to pay for IP ownership.

“The way we’ve approached it so far is they’re not owning the IP. But we can set up specifically owned assets within there, and they can have first right of refusal for future seasons,” Steele said.

Then if a show succeeds and there are future seasons, the brand can pick back up the IP ownership conversation.

“From a brand’s perspective, I think a lot of them aren’t ready to fully dive in with a full IP investment at that level because it is something that takes a long time to really come back and to grow. But from our standpoint, it’s fine because we approach it [by] pulling a little bit from the model of how our managers are currently doing brand deals with the talent and then also how we experience doing brand integrations from the traditional side. It’s just a lot more flexibility,” said Steele.

“When a federal agency formally recognizes millions of creator-led businesses, the people who decide where money goes start to treat creators as an asset class.”

#7352. With “Bimbo University,” Nikita Redkar leans into femininity to explain the news

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

“I’m the person who’s holding the door open and being like, ‘OK, you just got here, this is what we’re talking about right now.’”

The data is in: News creators and influencers are a major source of news for Americans, especially people under 30. This is the latest edition of Creators of Record, an occasional series of interviews with popular creators about how they do their jobs.

For Nikita Redkar, a big part of filming a video is putting together an outfit.

In a July video about youth-led protests in India, the 33-year-old is walking around New York City explaining how movements grow when people with opposing views come together on the things they do agree on. She’s wearing a white tube top and black jeans with sunglasses holding her hair back, along with a matching gold necklace and earrings that look like soda can tabs. You can’t see her shoes, but they clack enough for you to know she’s wearing a heel.

In another about comedian Shane Gillis’s Saturday Night Live monologue in which he called women “whores” for wearing shorts to the gym, she’s seated on the floor of her living room wearing a black silk velvet top with a pink faux-fur collar and sleeve cuffs. In the style of a Get Ready With Me video, she explains how 2000s comedy and pop culture entrenched misogynistic ideas of gender roles while applying her makeup.

A full-time news creator who mostly focuses on American politics and culture through a leftist and feminist lens, Redkar calls herself a “professor at Bimbo University.” The girliness is the point, she told me.

“I want to show that you don’t have to sacrifice these parts of yourself to be taken seriously,” Redkar said. “You should be taken seriously no matter what.”

Redkar and I chatted in October about how she does her job, leaning into her femininity to explain the news, and what her relationship with social media is like. Our conversation has been edited for length and clarity.

Hanaa’ Tameez: How did you start creating news videos? Nikita Redkar: I started in 2022, kind of accidentally. I did standup comedy for five years before the pandemic. I was always doing political humor. I took many political science classes in college, I wrote for the college newspaper, and I always had an interest in politics. In 2022, I had a video that really took off — like millions of views within a few hours. I was explaining the Russian invasion of Ukraine with a silly metaphor about Russia being a jealous ex that doesn’t want to see his ex thrive and be independent. I tried that formula again a couple weeks later…about NATO and fears of World War III. I explained exactly what would be affected. I said there’s not going to be a world war, but things are going to get really expensive, which happened. I also used a girly friendship metaphor for that video. That’s when I started doing that formula — leaning into hyper-femininity to explain these complex geopolitical topics.

Hanaa’ Tameez: How did you start creating news videos? Nikita Redkar: I started in 2022, kind of accidentally. I did standup comedy for five years before the pandemic. I was always doing political humor. I took many political science classes in college, I wrote for the college newspaper, and I always had an interest in politics. In 2022, I had a video that really took off — like millions of views within a few hours. I was explaining the Russian invasion of Ukraine with a silly metaphor about Russia being a jealous ex that doesn’t want to see his ex thrive and be independent. I tried that formula again a couple weeks later…about NATO and fears of World War III. I explained exactly what would be affected. I said there’s not going to be a world war, but things are going to get really expensive, which happened. I also used a girly friendship metaphor for that video. That’s when I started doing that formula — leaning into hyper-femininity to explain these complex geopolitical topics.

Nikita Redkar: I started in 2022, kind of accidentally. I did standup comedy for five years before the pandemic. I was always doing political humor. I took many political science classes in college, I wrote for the college newspaper, and I always had an interest in politics.

In 2022, I had a video that really took off — like millions of views within a few hours. I was explaining the Russian invasion of Ukraine with a silly metaphor about Russia being a jealous ex that doesn’t want to see his ex thrive and be independent.

I tried that formula again a couple weeks later…about NATO and fears of World War III. I explained exactly what would be affected. I said there’s not going to be a world war, but things are going to get really expensive, which happened. I also used a girly friendship metaphor for that video. That’s when I started doing that formula — leaning into hyper-femininity to explain these complex geopolitical topics.

Tameez: Tell me about why this work is important to you. Redkar: I feel like my work has made a lot of these daunting topics a little bit more accessible. I incorporate a lot of humor, which is important because there’s a lot of doom and gloom on social media. It’s a normal reaction to have toward everything that’s going on, and I feel it myself, too. But when I talk to my audience, I intentionally give them hope. I don’t just tell the news, but tell them, OK, if we really locked in and held these corporations and the government accountable and we were intentional in our activism, this is what the world could look like. It’s important to make information accessible because we are going through so much news-wise and not all of it is easy to understand. I enjoy bringing this information I’m naturally interested in to people in a way where everyone [can] get involved. When I was starting, I was not [a stereotypical] history buff or a news junkie. I would like to think that someone who looks like me and talks like me should be just as interested in politics and just as interested in the outcome of this country.

Redkar: I feel like my work has made a lot of these daunting topics a little bit more accessible. I incorporate a lot of humor, which is important because there’s a lot of doom and gloom on social media. It’s a normal reaction to have toward everything that’s going on, and I feel it myself, too. But when I talk to my audience, I intentionally give them hope. I don’t just tell the news, but tell them, OK, if we really locked in and held these corporations and the government accountable and we were intentional in our activism, this is what the world could look like.

It’s important to make information accessible because we are going through so much news-wise and not all of it is easy to understand. I enjoy bringing this information I’m naturally interested in to people in a way where everyone [can] get involved.

When I was starting, I was not [a stereotypical] history buff or a news junkie. I would like to think that someone who looks like me and talks like me should be just as interested in politics and just as interested in the outcome of this country.

Tameez: Tell me more about that. You have a little bit of an online persona. Redkar: That was the whole reason I created Bimbo University. I wanted to get more women on board with being interested in politics and history in particular, because history is seen as such a male-dominated [field]. It was really important to me to bring, colloquially speaking, the girls and the gays and the theys into the space, because it is fascinating and it provides context to what we’re going through right now. It makes the present a little less daunting when you study historical cycles and realize that people 100 years ago dealt with [similar things to what we’re going through now]. How did they come out of that?

Redkar: That was the whole reason I created Bimbo University. I wanted to get more women on board with being interested in politics and history in particular, because history is seen as such a male-dominated [field].

It was really important to me to bring, colloquially speaking, the girls and the gays and the theys into the space, because it is fascinating and it provides context to what we’re going through right now. It makes the present a little less daunting when you study historical cycles and realize that people 100 years ago dealt with [similar things to what we’re going through now]. How did they come out of that?

Tameez: How do you define your niche and how did you get there? Redkar: My niche is educational. Bimbo University is a place where anyone can start no matter where they are [in their political education]. My audience [ranges from] people who are just getting into politics to people who maybe were triggered by a specific event in the news to start paying more attention to it. I feel like leftist content creators all operate on a spectrum. You have people who are getting really deep into Marxist theory, for example, and things like geopolitics. I’m at the beginning. I’m the person who’s holding the door open and being like, “OK, you just got here, this is what we’re talking about right now.”

Redkar: My niche is educational. Bimbo University is a place where anyone can start no matter where they are [in their political education]. My audience [ranges from] people who are just getting into politics to people who maybe were triggered by a specific event in the news to start paying more attention to it.

I feel like leftist content creators all operate on a spectrum. You have people who are getting really deep into Marxist theory, for example, and things like geopolitics. I’m at the beginning. I’m the person who’s holding the door open and being like, “OK, you just got here, this is what we’re talking about right now.”

Tameez: You’re the first news creator I’ve talked to who does Get Ready With Me videos. You take all of your interests and apply them [to your videos] to be relatable. Redkar: I got into this huge argument with my best friend’s mom, who was a second-wave feminist in the 70s and 80s. She was upset that I called my page Bimbo University. She was like, “You’re dumbing yourself down. Women shouldn’t dumb themselves down. You are just as smart as a man, if not smarter than a man.” That was my lightbulb moment of, oh, your feminism has always been to measure your worth as equal to a man and by the standards of men…working toward a man’s acceptance. In some industries, you’ve got to do that. I don’t blame [people] for thinking like that. But the reason I dial so deep into this hyper-femininity — leaning into these girly things like getting ready with me or walking with my outfit showing — is because I want to show that you don’t have to sacrifice those parts of yourself to be taken seriously. You should be taken seriously no matter what.

She was upset that I called my page Bimbo University. She was like, “You’re dumbing yourself down. Women shouldn’t dumb themselves down. You are just as smart as a man, if not smarter than a man.”

That was my lightbulb moment of, oh, your feminism has always been to measure your worth as equal to a man and by the standards of men…working toward a man’s acceptance. In some industries, you’ve got to do that. I don’t blame [people] for thinking like that.

But the reason I dial so deep into this hyper-femininity — leaning into these girly things like getting ready with me or walking with my outfit showing — is because I want to show that you don’t have to sacrifice those parts of yourself to be taken seriously. You should be taken seriously no matter what.

A post shared by Nikita Redkar (@nikitadumptruck)

Redkar: On Mondays, I have a researcher and we go through the topics that people are talking about on social media. We pull up some news sites and see what the big stories are, and we leave some room for breaking news throughout the week.

My week is [split] between videos [on topics] that the general public is talking about, and videos that are just interesting to me. That’s how I stay sane in this business. I’m lucky to have the flexibility of doing that. My researcher combs through news sites and research papers and compiles all that information for me, then I put together a script.

Redkar: It’s definitely my favorite color, but it’s like the chicken and the egg — I don’t know if I liked it before I was doing this.

I pick whatever outfit I feel cute in. In getting ready with me videos, I start with a completely bare face, so at least the outfit has to be giving a little bit.

Tameez: Tell me about the Get Ready with Me videos. How do you decide what’s a GRWM and what’s a walking video?

Redkar: It’s like: What am I comfortable screaming out in the streets versus what do I need to keep at home? Some topics require more seriousness; if it does, I can do the GRWM for that. But if it’s something [less serious], I can be walking my dog or have on some ridiculous outfit.

Redkar: If I get a fact wrong, I’ll do a follow-up video. It’s rare that I’ve gotten a fact wrong because between me and my researcher, that’s two levels of checks before something hits the internet.

For one video, I didn’t factually get it wrong, but I should have included more of the story. It was about the Ugandan dictator Idi Amin and how Indians were expelled from Uganda, which is kind of how we got both Zohran Mamdani and Charli XCX [living in the U.S.], because both of their parents were part of the group that was expelled. I disregarded the racism that local Ugandans faced because of the Indians. I just focused on the expulsion and I framed Indians as the victims.

A lot of people in the comments were like, “Hey, like, you really should have said this other part too.” I just deleted that video, because I didn’t want this to spread and only give half of the story.

Redkar: My favorite video in recent memory was about girls on the first date, how we’re all so vigilant these days about dating men. I had so much fun doing it because I was talking about how life is affected by politics, instead of just politics. I think that video did really well because a lot of people connected to it.

Last year I did a video that I called “How we went from Brat Summer to Hitler Winter” and talked about the hedonism of the 2024 summer and the fascism that followed. I related it to the Roaring 20s transitioning to the Great Depression to the World War. I incorporated a lot of pop culture [references].

A post shared by Nikita Redkar (@nikitadumptruck)

Redkar: Constantly. I posted a video about the Cornell professor who invented the term sexual harassment. I thought it was so interesting and timely, but it did not do well at all. The problem with content creation is sometimes you can’t tell if people didn’t like a video or if you’re being shadow banned.

Redkar: I really love Liz Plank. She’s @feministabulous on Instagram. I love her coverage and how she leans into current conversations in pop culture.

I really like Charles McBryde. He’s not talking about just news — he’s very foreign policy-focused as well.

@MissEatingGood on Instagram is a great explainer of what’s going on and the historical context.

Redkar: Just finding ways to work together. I think there is so much benefit in that. I know countless creators who take a journalist’s article and dissect it down. Maybe [the journalists] should be the ones doing that.

I would happily pair up with journalists and teach them the ins and outs of content creation and how to make a short-form video article that’s also attention-grabbing. That’s just the reality; people are on social media, everyone’s attention span is shot, which sucks. We could try to fix that, but at this moment, we’re not fixing it. It’s really helpful to go on social media and talk [about your reporting] because journalists work hard on their articles.

Creators [should] take more time. We rush way too much. It’s not our fault, because these platforms reward you for making content more frequently and punish you if you take a few days off. [Not rushing] sucks, but what can you do? It’s better than putting incorrect, or rushed, or incomplete information out there.

#7351. Customer Solution Architect (AU, NZ, SG) at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re seeking a Customer Solutions Architect (CSA) to help drive the adoption of Supabase across a diverse range of customers: from agile startups building towards their first launch to established brands with highly scaled workloads.

At Supabase, CSA’s are responsible for the entire customer journey, working closely with our most valuable users to:

Uncover and understand business and technical requirements.

Design and architect technical solutions built on the Supabase platform.

Work with customers to lead proof-of-value implementations.

Provide regular customer engagements to answer complex questions, unblock adoption, and optimize scaled performance.

Successful CSAs always put customers first, working closely with them to become trusted advisors who understand their needs, work as an extension of their team toward common success, and advocate on behalf of the customer within Supabase.

You'll be joining our team to support Supabase Team & Enterprise customers, where you will be expected to lead and innovate around the following areas of responsibility:

Lead technical discovery to uncover business and technical objectives, set appropriate expectations of the Supabase platform, and define the criteria for success, ensuring customers can receive the guidance they need to achieve it.

Design, build, and present Supabase platform demonstrations to high-value customers. Leverage quality storytelling to present the value of the Supabase platform and of the entire Supabase Success team.

Work directly with Growth team members to size deals, provide anticipated usage estimates, and identify potential workloads supported by the Platform.

Collaborate directly with customers to determine the technical scope of a proof-of-value to ensure customers recognize the value of the Supabase platform while minimizing risk to adoption.

Set proof-of-value timelines, business outcomes, and success criteria that ensure a customer is comfortable proceeding with the platform once a proof-of-value has been completed.

Actively guide new customers through an onboarding journey, utilizing emails, docs, guides, and webinars to promote their rapid deployment and growth on the platform.

Understand immediate custom goals (i.e onboard and adoption) as well as long-term customer opportunities (i.e. optimization & identifying new workloads) to build meaningful account plans for high-value customers.

Deliver on synchronous and asynchronous engagements with Supabase customers, including application architecture assessments, database design and performance optimization, and proactive support to enable customers to scale with confidence.

Serve as an internal champion for your customers and for the platform. Work closely with Support, Product, and Engineering to provide platform feedback, identify areas of improvement, while communicating platform roadmaps to customers.

Invest in customer connections to build a trust-based relationship. Everyday CSA’s work with the production data stores of startups and enterprises alike. Building trust in the Success team and Supabase platform is paramount to a successful customer relationship.

6+ years of relevant work experience in Solutions Architecture, Database Engineering, or similar.

Strong background with relational database management systems such as PostgreSQL or MySQL.

Background in web application development, with familiarity with Python, C# and popular JavaScript frameworks (React, Vue, Svelte) as well as Node.js.

Very strong communication skills, particularly of technical concepts. CSA’s at Supabase spend a significant amount of time translating business needs to technical outcomes, and technical concepts to organizational impact.

Well-organized and effective storyteller with meaningful interpersonal skills. CSA’s often have to navigate multiple stakeholders with conflicting opinions, and the ability to effectively mediate this type of conflict is crucial.

Experience with project management, business analysis and revenue operations tools. We use HubSpot, BigQuery etc., and familiarity with these platforms will help enable your success.

Previous experience leading customer-facing engagements to deliver meaningful technical and business value.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

New remote jobs delivered to your inbox, every week.

Location independence is the future of work. It serves companies with best talent from around the globe. And benefits employees with a greater work life balance and the ability to work in their pyjamas.- Nithur, founder.

#7349. Meta pulls down controversial terms of service

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

“We’re pausing the changes as we continue to engage with stakeholders and take our time to get this right.”

Meta has indefinitely delayed its proposed commercial terms of service, which would have permitted the company to scrape the websites of Meta Business account users.

On Thursday, the company took down the preview link to the proposed terms and published an update to its Business Help Center that reads: “We recently shared that we were updating the Meta Commercial Terms, Product Catalog Terms, Self-Serve Ad Terms, and Ad Creative Generative AI Terms. These updates are now paused and will not take effect on November 20, 2026. We are continuing to consult with stakeholders and will provide a further update when we have more to share. In the meantime, our existing terms remain in effect.”

Meta — which owns Facebook, Messenger, Instagram, WhatsApp, and Threads — has repeatedly pushed back the terms’ effective date since Nieman Lab first reported on them on October 15. The terms were first set to take effect on October 30, sparking global outcry among news publishers, politicians, and trade organizations. On October 16, Meta removed the word “train” from its terms and limited the use of future scraped content to “advertising and business products.” On October 23, Meta moved the effective date back by three weeks to November 20.

Last week, Meta pledged to consult news publishers and industry leaders ahead of putting new commercial terms into effect.

“We’ve heard publishers’ feedback on our proposed ads and business terms update. We care deeply about our partners and do not plan to make policy changes without a full consultation with industry groups and leaders,” vice president of communications Andy Stone tweeted on October 28. “We’re committed to getting this right, and as we expect this consultation could extend beyond November 20, in which case we’d delay implementation of the updated terms.”

Since then, courts in Norway and Brazil have issued preliminary injunctions to block the terms from taking effect as a result of lawsuits filed by media organizations in both countries. The United Kingdom’s Competition and Markets Authority has opened a formal investigation into Meta, examining whether the company was “abusing a dominant position by imposing exploitative terms on publishers and advertisers.”

In the United States, the proposed terms stoked bipartisan concern. At the end of October, Sens. Adam Schiff (D-Calif.) and Marsha Blackburn (R-Tenn.) sent letters to Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg asking him to withdraw the terms.

#7348. November Click Spikes In Google Search Console Are Bot Traffic – How To Filter Your Reports via @sejournal, @marie_haynes

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Where search leaders are putting 2027 budget, plus the AI visibility scorecard that replaces session reporting.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

That sudden click spike in Search Console isn’t real traffic. Here’s what my data shows, what’s causing it, and how to filter it out.

Earlier this week, I saw Barry Schwartz’s post on X (and his write-up on Search Engine Roundtable) sharing a screenshot from Joy Hawkins. Joy noticed that several sites were seeing sudden, massive vertical spikes in Google Search Console clicks while impressions stayed flat, and Barry asked a great follow-up question: “Anyone else seeing this? If so, how are you filtering the report…”

Screenshot of Barry Schwartz and Joy Hawkins’ post on X highlighting the sudden click spike in Google Search Console. (Image Credit: Marie Haynes)

When I saw Barry’s tweet, I immediately thought this would be the ultimate real-world test for Marie’s Brain – my soon-to-be-launched agentic SEO and AI search analysis system.

Over the past several months, I have been building Marie’s Brain to combine my 15+ years of research on Google’s ranking systems with live, programmatic access to the Search Console and analytics data of the sites I monitor. Instead of manually clicking through Search Console property by property to see if any of my clients were affected, I asked my system to scan across all of the sites I monitor, check the unfinalized fresh data through Nov. 2, 2026, and flag any property showing a sudden anomaly in clicks.

Within seconds, Marie’s Brain surfaced the exact same vertical spike across multiple sites in completely different industries – and pulled the raw page, query, device, and country dimensions so I could see what was actually happening under the hood.

Just like Glenn Gabe and Joy Hawkins mentioned in Barry’s Search Engine Roundtable post, the majority of properties I monitor showed completely normal traffic. However, while some SEOs saw the wave begin over the weekend (Oct. 31 to Nov. 1), a distinct subset of sites I monitor experienced a dramatic vertical spike on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026 that mirrors Joy’s screenshot:

Six-month daily Google Search Console clicks (Search type: Web, including fresh unfinalized data through Nov. 2, 2026) across three affected properties I monitor. Image Credit: Marie Haynes

Here is how those three anonymized properties behaved on Nov. 2, 2026 compared to their prior seven-day baseline (Oct. 26 – Nov. 1, 2026):

When I had my system drill into the raw API rows for Nov. 2, two unmistakable patterns emerged across the affected sites:

Visual summary of why total impressions look flat while automated deep-SERP clicks create a vertical spike in Google Search Console. Image Credit: Marie Haynes

Normally, organic clicks in Search Console are heavily concentrated in the top 3 to 5 results on Page 1. On Nov. 2, click-through rate (CTR) exploded on pages ranking between Position 11 and Position 55 (Pages 2 through 6 of Google):

Why did total impressions stay flat if every click also had an impression? It comes down to simple scale. When a site normally gets 18,000 impressions and 50 clicks per day (like Site A), adding 275 automated impressions is only a 1.5% bump on the impression line – completely invisible on a chart! Adding 275 clicks to a 50-click baseline, on the other hand, creates a massive 6.5x vertical spike.

On Site C (a technical troubleshooting Q&A site), a single long-tail troubleshooting page that normally gets zero to one click per day (and never received more than seven clicks in a single day across the entire previous six months) suddenly jumped to 13 clicks on Nov. 1 and 40 clicks on Nov. 2.

While the spikes on Sites A and B came from U.S. searches, all 40 clicks on this Site C page came from Canada. From Oct. 28 to Oct.r 31, that page only had four to seven impressions per day in Canada (and 0 clicks) while ranking on Page 2. Then on Nov. 2, Canadian impressions on that page jumped by ~45 (to 50 impressions), and Canadian clicks jumped from 0 to 40 clicks – meaning almost every single one of those new impressions was clicked (an 80.0% CTR at average position 14.6 on Page 2, while U.S. clicks on that page stayed at 0)!

When I inspected the exact queries that clicked into that single URL on Nov. 2, there were 23 slightly reworded variations of the exact same troubleshooting question (including systematic number permutations like 3 times, 4 times, 5 times, 7 times, 10 times), almost every single one logging 100% CTR (1 click / 1 impression, 2/2, or 3/3) between Position 10.0 and Position 33.0. Other troubleshooting pages on the same site logged 1 click / 1 impression (100% CTR) at Position 45.5 to 54.0.

Normally, when a basic rank tracker or scraper loads a Google search results page, it only generates impressions – not clicks. For Search Console to record a click, a browser or bot has to actually follow (or trigger) Google’s click-tracking link on the search results page.

Looking at the data patterns across these sites, four possibilities stand out:

Because GSC does not let you filter the top performance chart by ranking position or CTR, there is no perfect one-click button to strip only the bot clicks out of your sitewide graph if dozens of pages were affected. However, these three approaches work well depending on how your site was impacted:

If dozens of pages across your whole site spiked on Desktop (like Sites A & B), you cannot exclude every page individually. While filtering the top bar by Device: Mobile obviously hides your legitimate Desktop traffic too, it is a great sanity check to show a client or stakeholder that your Mobile organic trendline is completely normal (on Site A, Mobile clicks were 30 on Nov 2 vs. 27-40 on prior days; on Site B, Mobile clicks were 11 on Nov 2 vs. 11 on Nov 1).

To see the exact pages that were affected on your site:

If you have Claude, Gemini, ChatGPT, or an agentic coding environment (like Antigravity or Cursor) connected to the Google Search Console API or a custom MCP server, you can paste this exact prompt to check all of your sites in seconds:

No. Because this is automated activity rather than human visitors, you do not need to change your pages, disavow anything, or panic when those clicks drop right back down to baseline. Just annotate early November 2026 in your reporting (right alongside GSC’s other recent reporting changes) so you and your clients know why Search Console suddenly showed a vertical jump in clicks!

#7347. What DoorDash’s Instacart acquisition says about the modern delivery platform pitch

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This story was first published by Digiday sibling Modern Retail.

These days, delivery platforms have to offer more than just delivery services to brands and retailers.

DoorDash’s announced plans to acquire Instacart for $17 billion point to the constant need to gain market share by diversifying services and offerings.

Over the years, both DoorDash and Instacart have invested heavily in new services like AI assistants, advertising programs and in-store B-to-B solutions. DoorDash, in particular, has been on an expansion streak, acquiring U.K.-based competitor Deliveroo and restaurant reservations platform SevenRooms in 2025. Last year, DoorDash also acquired search ad startup Symbiosys for $175 million to deepen its advertising capabilities.

With the high cost of last-mile delivery, companies like DoorDash and Instacart have expanded well beyond delivering groceries and takeout.

Leah Sallen, U.S. head of CPG and retail strategy at programmatic ad tech platform MiQ, said the growing services and tools DoorDash has come to provide have made it more than just a marketplace for delivery. “Between delivery, grocery shopping, restaurant reservations and advertising, DoorDash is gaining access to more consumer touchpoints and insights into when, where and how people shop,” she said.

Over the past few years, DoorDash has aggressively built out its non-restaurant marketplace, partnering with major grocery, convenience and retail brands like Ulta, Skims and Macy’s. The company also introduced logistics features to solve pain points, like Dasher Returns. On the technology side, DoorDash has rolled out AI-powered tools, such as Ask DoorDash for shopping recommendations, DashBuddy for courier support and DashOS for restaurant customer management. These solutions are meant to create an all-in-one ecosystem for B-to-B infrastructure and logistics streamlining.

Now, the Instacart consolidation will bring DoorDash grocery retailers that the platform currently does not offer its customers, notably Walmart and Sam’s Club. But with that market share also comes challenges in maintaining retail relationships by offering more than just delivery solutions.

Neil Saunders, managing director of GlobalData, said DoorDash’s latest hefty acquisition will require further streamlining to make sense as a long-term investment.

Saunders said Instacart’s operations still largely rely on workers manually picking groceries. He said this is not only labor-intensive, but it’s also harder to scale efficiently as digital grocery orders continue to grow. “Either way, DoorDash should aim to position itself as a technology and infrastructure partner rather than simply a delivery provider,” Saunders said.

Some experts believe DoorDash’s proactive strategy is to simply keep up with Amazon’s delivery offerings. However, retail media networks have also become an important revenue source for delivery platforms in recent years.

At its surface, the DoorDash-Instacart deal is about two massive last-mile companies consolidating. But for advertisers, it could mean a broader disruption in retail loyalty, said Sallen, as the two platforms’ merger brings together deep data on restaurant, grocery and retail shopping.

“We have already seen this play out with TikTok Shop, which fundamentally changed consumer shopping behavior by using sophisticated algorithms to deliver highly personalized product recommendations,” Sallen said. A similar shift can happen with this acquisition, Sallen said, thanks to the combination of DoorDash’s immediate-consumption insights and Instacart’s long-standing grocery data.

Sallen explained that, similar to how Amazon became a starting point for shopping, consumers may increasingly turn to platforms like DoorDash to find the best product, price or delivery option before deciding which retailer to purchase from. In turn, she said, “That shift from selecting the retailer first to selecting the retailer last could prompt CPG brands to reconsider how they allocate their retail media dollars.”

Matt Gilbert, CEO of marketing platform Partnerize, agreed that the significance of a DoorDash and Instacart combination goes well beyond delivery.

“It’s about the growing concentration of consumer discovery, advertising, purchasing decisions and transaction data within a single commerce ecosystem,” Gilbert said. For consumers, that could mean greater convenience and fewer steps between discovering a product and buying it. “For advertisers, it creates an opportunity to connect marketing investment more directly to purchasing behavior across more categories and occasions.”

On the other hand, Gilbert said that when platforms increasingly control the consumer experience, the transaction and the reporting of advertising performance, advertisers need assurance that they’re measuring genuine incremental influence — “and not simply taking credit for purchases that would have happened anyway,” said Gilbert.

Sallen added that, as platforms like DoorDash add capabilities and AI increasingly influences shopping decisions, “The consumer path to purchase is becoming more diluted and harder for advertisers to follow.”

That shift could become even more pronounced as AI agents increasingly help consumers weigh price, proximity and product availability before deciding what and where to buy. Sallen said this could mean delivery behemoths like DoorDash could have an advantage in shaping AI-driven recommendations to millions of consumers. “The more intelligence it [DoorDash] can bring together, the more personalized it can make the shopping experience,” Sallen said.

“DoorDash’s growing portfolio of services could position it as a one-stop shop for retailers, with data becoming one of its biggest advantages,” Sallen added.

Publishers are pushing back against Meta’s AI scraping terms, testing their leverage over a platform they still rely on.

Political and news creators are relied on now more than ever, but what platforms are working for them? Which ones aren’t?

#7344. Digiday+ Research 2026 Creator Marketing Index: A brand guide to social media campaigns

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This research is based on unique data collected from our proprietary audience of publisher, agency, brand and tech insiders. It’s available to Digiday+ members. More from the series →

As creator marketing becomes a fixture in many brands’ marketing strategies, creators’ roles are shifting and expanding. Brands may pay creators to produce sponsored content, gift products to them in the hope of organic posts, or rely on a combination of creator- and brand-produced content published on their own social media accounts.

Overall, social media remains the leading channel for creator partnerships: 96% of respondents to Digiday’s first-quarter survey of brands and agencies said they partner with creators to create social media content. Within social media, Instagram (90%), Facebook (80%), YouTube (72%) and TikTok (63%) were the platforms marketers said they used most.

Across these social platforms, brands are taking various approaches to creator campaigns, from sponsored and gifted posts to content formats like product demonstrations, styled photos and event coverage. Understanding how these approaches perform can help brands make informed decisions about creator partnerships, content formats and campaign investment.

To measure the impact creator content has on brand campaigns, Digiday+ Research analyzed the engagement levels on leading creators’ social media campaigns from January to September 2026.

The Digiday Creator Marketing Index collected data from 1,755 social media posts and 20 branded social media campaigns, analyzing the top posts associated with each campaign’s hashtag and scoring them across a set of key dimensions to create a total index average score. Results are dependent upon the list of social media campaigns and the time period of data collection, generating a snapshot of the creator marketing landscape at a specific moment in time.

Digiday’s Index collected social media data from January to September 2026. Due to data restrictions, TikTok views were not captured. Data collection was conducted by Dash Social, an agentic social platform powered by brand-intelligent AI.

The social media campaigns were selected by Digiday’s editorial team.

When considering video content performance by platform, Instagram Reels received far more video views than YouTube’s Shorts and long-form videos, while TikTok videos had higher average engagement in later analysis. However, these results should be considered within the context of the Index’s focus on branded social media campaigns, meaning that the majority of posts included marketing-focused content rather than non-brand-related content, which performs the best on YouTube. Additionally, on YouTube — a video-first platform with a core strength in long-form content — most of the captured posts consisted of Shorts rather than long-form posts.

Average engagement rate — engagement divided by follower count — was only measurable on Instagram. When looking at average engagement rate by format type on Instagram, Reels performed the best among Instagram’s formats at an 11% engagement rate, underscoring the strength of short-form video on Instagram. Still posts came in second with a 7% engagement rate, followed by Carousel posts at 5%. Carousel posts can contain videos, but views were not counted as part of the average engagement rate for Carousel posts with video. Likewise, views were not counted as part of the average engagement rate for Still posts or Reels.

The top 75 posts associated with each campaign’s hashtag were identified based on overall engagement, including likes and comments across platforms. When Digiday analyzed those top-performing posts by platform, with engagement weighted equally across platforms, Instagram accounted for the largest share of posts associated with campaign hashtags.

However, TikTok posts within the top 75 generated higher average engagement than posts on other platforms. In other words, Instagram appeared more frequently among the highest-performing posts overall, while the top-performing TikTok posts generated greater engagement on average.

According to Digiday’s analysis, Instagram is a more consistent platform for performance, while TikTok has greater reach potential and a higher engagement ceiling. For brands running longer campaigns or prioritizing consistency, Instagram offers a more predictable environment for creator marketing. For smaller brands or those with limited budgets, TikTok is likely the better platform choice for creator marketing. While engagement on TikTok isn’t as consistent, its higher engagement ceiling gives creator content a better possibility of reaching a wider audience.

Looking more closely at sponsored and gifted posts, the Index defines them as follows. Sponsored posts refer to content created through a direct partnership between a brand and a creator. Gifted posts feature products that brands have sent to creators, typically as part of a PR effort, without a paid content agreement. Digiday classified posts as sponsored or gifted based on disclosures in captions, including paid labels, disclaimers, and hashtags such as #ad, #paid, #gifted and #PR. In some video posts, creators verbally expressed whether they had been paid by a brand.

The most common content types across both sponsored posts and gifted posts were product demonstrations and style shots. The Index defines a demo as content about a single brand or product that includes a description and shows a person using the item. A style shot is content that showcases a product in a scenic or visually styled setting without an explanation of how to use the item.

Style shots appeared more frequently in gifted posts than they did in sponsored posts, by a ratio of 19 to 15, indicating that creators favor that format when featuring products they have received for free without a formal content agreement. One possible explanation is that demos can appear to be overtly promotional since they feature one product as opposed to a “get ready with me” (GRWM) format that typically showcases multiple brands. Creators may want to avoid producing content that could be interpreted as advertising when it comes to gifted items, according to Digiday’s analysis.

When comparing average engagement rates, sponsored posts outperformed gifted posts across content types, with one notable exception: demos  — the most used content format type — where gifted posts performed best. Style shots displayed the opposite pattern, performing better in sponsored content where they also delivered the highest overall engagement among the content formats analyzed.

One potential factor behind this difference is the level of production and associated costs behind the content. Gifted style shots were often simple flat lays or product images, while sponsored style shots frequently included product videos. Brands often give creators larger budgets to develop content for sponsored product posts. That distinction is particularly important for product categories like fragrance, where brands rely on visual storytelling to communicate abstract qualities like mood and sensory experience.

While engagement provides a strong measure of how audiences respond to creator content, reach — calculated by average views — offers another important metric of performance. Digiday’s analysis found that sponsored posts had greater overall reach based on views than gifted posts, by a margin of 334,055 to 48,008. Within sponsored posts, style shots (707,646), demos (250,764) and influencer events (231,595) had the most views. Among gifted posts, demos generated the most views (81,023), well below sponsored formats.

That margin of difference is likely also linked to budget differences, according to Digiday’s analysis. Brands typically provide creators with paid media budgets to amplify sponsored posts beyond a creator’s existing audience, while gifted posts are limited to a creator’s own audience. As a result, sponsored content can generate both organic and paid reach, while gifted product content relies on organic distribution with more limited reach.

This Index exclusively analyzes brand-generated hashtags that incorporated either a brand name or a trademarked product name, such as #exaggereyes, #chocolatesoleil and #hydroboost.

The majority of the highest-ranked campaigns had a strong social media presence with a significant number of brand posts on the brand-owned handle, in addition to creator posts. By contrast, lower-ranked campaigns had a weaker presence on brand-owned channels, with no brand-generated posts in many cases. These findings suggest that while creator participation is an important driver of campaign success, brands’ own social media activity also plays a critical role, according to Digiday’s analysis.

Many of the highest-ranked campaigns also included a mix of gifted posts and sponsored posts. Gifted posts came from both macro- and micro-creators, indicating that high-ranked campaigns sent products to a wide range of content creators. However, investment was another important factor. All of the highest-ranked campaigns had large budgets backing them, highlighting the growing role of pay-to-play social media campaigns, where brands must invest in both creator partnerships and amplification to build reach.

Not all campaigns with paid support performed equally, however, suggesting that investment alone does not guarantee stronger performance.

Charlotte Tilbury’s #exaggereyes campaign ranked No. 1 in Digiday’s Index. One of its defining characteristics was the use of gifted posts: 35% of the campaign’s posts came from creators who received gifted products, the highest share among the campaigns Digiday analyzed.

Charlotte Tilbury’s collaboration with Influenster, a product sampling and review platform owned by Bazaarvoice, generated a large portion of the gifted posts for the #exaggereyes campaign. Rather than selecting creators and sending them PR packages directly, the brand relied on Influenster to collect user-generated content for its own use. Most of the gifted posts were demo videos, a format that Digiday’s analysis found generated higher average engagement within gifted posts than sponsored posts.

Neutrogena’s #hydroboost campaign ranked No. 2 in the Index and focused on a product-line extension. Neutrogena heavily invested in sending gifted products and directly collaborating with creators. As a result, the Hydro Boost campaign had the highest share of brand-seeded content — sponsored or gifted posts — at 39% of all posts.

The campaign also incorporated the broader #hyaluronicacid hashtag alongside the branded #hyrdoboost hashtag. This strategy allowed Neutrogena to not only position the Hydro Boost product as a line extension but also connect the campaign to the larger hyaluronic acid product category, potentially exposing the campaign to audiences with a broader interest in hyaluronic acid products.

Like Neutrogena’s campaign, Too Faced’s #chocolatesoleil campaign promoted a product-line extension but with a focus on the Brazilian market. Thirty-four of the top 75 posts associated with the Chocolate Soleil campaign hashtag mentioned Brazil, including content from Too Faced’s Brazilian social media account and other Brazilian creator accounts, indicating a deliberate focus on localizing the campaign for that market.

The campaign also had the lowest share of sponsored or gifted posts among the 10 highest-ranked campaigns, with 74 of its top 75 posts classified as organic posts. Instead, the campaign focused heavily on the product launch and activity generated through Too Faced’s brand handle to entice creators to test the product and create content about it.

Taken together, these campaigns show that there is no single formula for a successful brand-generated hashtag campaign. High-performing campaigns combined product seeding, creator partnerships, brand-owned content, organic activity and localized strategies in different ways. According to Digiday’s analysis, brand-generated hashtags can give brands greater ownership of the campaign conversation, particularly when tied to a specific product or launch, but their narrower focus may limit the volume and variety of content generated around them.

OpenAI has ambitious ad revenue targets, which depend on advertiser demand, measurement and monetisation.

Agencies and marketers now frequently use AI tools to create media and creative briefs. But the practice could be a canary in the coalmine for AI overuse in advertising.

The DoorDash-Instacart deal could accelerate commerce media consolidation, promising scale but risking higher prices and lock-in.

#7343. Google Search Queries Hit A 5-Year High, And Search Revenue Didn’t Follow via @sejournal, @DuaneForrester

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Where search leaders are putting 2027 budget, plus the AI visibility scorecard that replaces session reporting.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Alphabet’s Q3 numbers show AI Mode growing faster than it earns, and every reason Google gave for the slowdown is temporary except one.

Alphabet just reported the fastest growth in Google search queries in five years; in the same quarter, its search revenue grew at the slowest rate in 2026.

Roger covered the earnings call earlier this week on Search Engine Journal, and it’s a great overview of the high points. I’m diving deeper to make a specific point here.

Search & other revenue grew 12% to $63.35 billion in the third quarter, according to Alphabet’s earnings release, down from 19% in the first quarter and 17% in the second. That was roughly $1.6 billion below analyst expectations, and CNBC reported it was the second straight quarter search came in under estimates. On the earnings call, Google’s chief business officer, Philipp Schindler, named one of the causes directly: strong growth in AI Mode queries “had an impact because of the lower monetization we were seeing in AI Mode.”

For two years, the argument over whether AI answers cut into search has run on inferred numbers: click-through studies, panel estimates, and referral samples, many from vendors with something to sell. This quarter, the effect shows up in a counted number, Google’s own revenue line, with a Google executive naming AI Mode as a cause. That changes the quality of the evidence, even if it doesn’t finalize the argument.

A quick disclosure before going further: I run a company that measures visibility inside AI answers, so a story about money moving into those answers isn’t neutral ground for me.

The market reaction was severe. Alphabet shares fell about 13% on Thursday, the steepest one-day drop on record, erasing roughly half a trillion dollars in market value. Search wasn’t the only reason. Free cash flow came in at negative $9.07 billion against an expected shortfall of about $2.7 billion, and capital spending is still climbing. So I won’t claim investors sold because they rejected Google’s explanation for search. What I will claim is that the explanation needs a closer read than it’s getting, because the part that matters most is the part Google spent the least time on.

Schindler listed four drivers of the slowdown from the second quarter to the third. Foreign exchange came first, and he called it the single largest driver of the search revenue deceleration. Second, some retail advertisers in Asia-Pacific pulled back after the EU eliminated its de minimis exemption for low-value imports. Third, media and entertainment spending normalized after a second quarter inflated by the FIFA World Cup. Fourth, AI Mode queries grew quickly and earn less than traditional search. CFO Anat Ashkenazi added that Google has started lapping the search acceleration that began in the third quarter of 2025, which makes year-over-year comparisons harder for a few quarters.

Put those drivers on a timeline, and they sort themselves. A currency swing reverses or gets absorbed. The World Cup is over. A trade-rule change gets priced into advertiser budgets within a few quarters. Lapping a strong year is arithmetic that fades on its own. AI Mode is the only factor on the list that gets larger each quarter, because Google is actively growing it, and the lower monetization comes along with every query it adds.

Google’s Q3 explanation for slower search revenue named three temporary factors and one structural one: AI Mode queries are growing faster than they earn, and that gap widens with every quarter of adoption until ad yield inside the answer catches up.

I understand the instinct to dismiss the currency explanation as convenient. I’d rather check it, because Alphabet publishes enough to do that. Total revenue grew 25% in the third quarter whether or not you adjust for currency; the constant-currency reconciliation tables in the release show it, and Ashkenazi described FX as a slight headwind on year-over-year growth. In the second quarter, revenue grew 24%, or 23% in constant currency, which means currency added about a point. Across the whole company, then, currency moved from a slight tailwind to a slight headwind, a swing of a point or two at most.

Ashkenazi also said FX had a more significant impact on a sequential basis, and the release bears that out: Revenue grew 6% from the second quarter as reported and 7% in constant currency. That’s a real effect. It’s also a one-point effect, and it applies to the company as a whole, not to search specifically.

Search growth fell five points over the same period. Google doesn’t publish a constant-currency figure for search alone, so this can’t be determined precisely. But unless search is far more exposed to currency than the rest of Alphabet, my estimate is that FX accounts for something like one to two of those five points. That makes it plausible as the single largest driver, and it still leaves most of the slowdown to the other factors combined.

That distinction matters. “Single largest driver” is a ranking. It tells you FX beat each of the other causes individually. It doesn’t tell you FX explains most of the decline, and it says nothing about which causes will still be there next year. The currency explanation is probably true. It just isn’t the answer to the question investors and practitioners are actually asking, which is whether the new way people search with Google earns as much as the old one.

The other headline number deserves the same scrutiny. Sundar Pichai said Google’s year-over-year query growth rate is the highest it has been in five years. Google didn’t give the rate, and it didn’t say how a query is counted inside an AI Mode session. According to the call transcript, AI Mode queries average three times the length of traditional searches, and Google is now running multi-turn conversations in AI Mode, with ads designed for them. If each turn in a conversation counts as a query, query growth rises mechanically as conversations get longer, whether or not more people are searching for more things.

I’m not claiming that’s how Google counts. I’m saying the record query growth is a counted number with a definition we can’t see, and that kind of number should be held loosely until the definition is published. Revenue is much harder to redefine, which is why the revenue line is where the AI Mode story finally became visible.

In my forecast earlier in October that the SERP stops being Google’s default between 2028 and 2030, I argued the trigger is ad yield. Google flips the default when an answer earns roughly what a results page earns per query, because before that point flipping costs money and after it keeping the old default does. One of the signals I said would push the timeline later was weak returns from AI Mode ad placements.

Q3 is Google confirming, in its own revenue, that parity hasn’t arrived. That fits the forecast’s explanation for why Google isn’t in a hurry to flip anything. It also tells you where Google’s effort goes next. Google isn’t going to slow AI Mode down to protect the results page; Pichai credits AI for the query growth, and the company is plainly committed to it. Instead, Google closes the yield gap by putting more advertising inside the answer.

The call laid that plan out. Schindler said Google is scaling ads within both AI Overviews and AI Mode to more users and more queries, taking AI Mode ads international for the first time, and introducing multi-turn ads in AI Mode in the U.S. He also said Google’s ad experiments have shown promising results, a phrase that will either become a reported metric in coming quarters or quietly disappear.

None of this surprised me. In August 2025, I described an ad system that builds the ad at query time inside the response, with advertisers supplying inputs rather than finished creative, and placement decided by how closely a brand aligns with the intent behind the prompt. Google shipped an early version at Google Marketing Live in May. Q3 explains why the rest of that design is likely to arrive faster: Google now has a quarterly revenue reason to finish building it.

One line from the call should get more attention from SEOs than it will. Schindler said click-through rates “are already similar between sponsored and organic product list items within AI Mode.” On a traditional results page, organic and paid listings occupied different territory with different click behavior, and practitioners built entire strategies around that separation. Inside an AI Mode answer, by Google’s own account, a sponsored product listing and an organic one draw clicks at about the same rate.

Put that next to the yield gap and my read is straightforward, though I want to be clear it’s my opinion and not something Google said. An answer has a small number of places where a brand can appear. As Google adds ads to close the gap, more of those places become available for purchase, and if sponsored and organic placements perform alike, advertisers have every reason to buy them. Organic visibility inside the answer doesn’t disappear, but it now competes for space with inventory that carries a direct revenue target.

Google also said the majority of AI Mode queries are incremental, and that many are upper-funnel queries carrying new commercial intent. That’s Google’s claim, and nobody outside Google can verify it. If it’s accurate, AI Mode is creating commercial moments that never existed on the results page, and the decision about which brands appear in them happens inside the answer, before anyone clicks anything. That’s a strong argument for measuring presence in the answer and not just clicks out of it.

The practical move is to establish a baseline now, before ad load inside AI Mode scales. For the queries that matter most in your category, record which answers cite you, which cite competitors, and which already carry sponsored units alongside the citations. A quarter from now, that record tells you whether paid placements are taking positions organic sources used to hold, and it gives you evidence to bring to budget conversations instead of an impression. Google will report its side of this story in aggregate. Nobody is going to report yours for you.

This is where the difference between counted and inferred numbers stops being academic. Google’s revenue line is counted and audited. The query growth claim is counted but undefined. The incremental-query claim is asserted. Click-through studies of AI answers are inferred from samples. Each deserves a different level of trust, and the mistake I see most often is treating them as interchangeable because they all arrive as percentages. Measuring how brands actually show up inside AI answers, rather than inferring it from traffic, is the problem I built CitationIQ to address, and it becomes harder and more necessary as paid placements fill those answers.

Google’s fourth-quarter report arrives in early February, and Ashkenazi has already said FX will be a slightly larger headwind and lapping will cost a couple of points of growth. Q4 comes with the same cover built in. Here’s what I’ll be looking at instead:

The third-quarter report is the first time the gap between how much people use Google’s AI search and how much it earns appeared in Google’s own revenue, and Google’s response to that gap will be more advertising inside AI answers, where paid and organic placements now compete for the same small set of positions.
