#7340. WTF is scene-level targeting for CTV?

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This WTF guide, sponsored by Wurl, explores how CTV advertisers are driving positive attention and improved performance across the entire funnel by aligning creative with relevant onscreen context through scene-level targeting.

CTV has changed how advertisers reach audiences through the biggest screen in the home.

Research shows that ad-supported streaming TV services are experiencing a surge in popularity. Despite this boon in both viewership and content supply, CTV advertisers still struggle with an opaque ecosystem that limits the ability to control where their ads appear. On top of this, ad fill rates aren’t quite keeping pace, according to Wurl — signaling a significant opportunity for advertisers and content owners alike. This dynamic is ripe for change, with new tools and technologies now available that promise to help advertisers reach engaged viewers more effectively and efficiently at scale.

Among the new tools is scene-level targeting, a form of contextual advertising that analyzes content on screen just before the ad break to determine which ad should appear next. With scene-level targeting on CTV, advertisers can pinpoint relevant moments for greater impact.

“Effective communication often relies on the context in which it is delivered; the same rules apply in advertising. Scene-level targeting introduces a whole new level of precision to TV advertising,” said Ria Madrid, vice president of global advertising at Wurl. “Marketers can now deliver their brand messaging adjacent to relevant content and in alignment with viewers’ mindsets and level of attentiveness. It creates a smarter, more prescriptive connection between brands and consumers.”

In this WTF explainer guide, Digiday and Wurl examine what scene-level targeting is, how it differs from other targeting strategies and how brands are implementing it.

Scene-level targeting considers what is happening on screen in a show or movie before an ad break — not just the app, channel, genre or audience. By focusing on what is being watched and when — such as the emotional tone or the brand safety of a specific scene — scene-level targeting places ads in compatible moments that feel relevant to viewers.

Advertisers commonly rely solely on audience targeting, which is based on household-level IDs, or on broad, traditional genre targeting that does not account for nuanced moments. But with scene-level signals, brands can better align their creative with the context of a given scene, even if they continue to apply traditional strategies like audience targeting.

Broad targeting treats every scene as equal, when really they aren’t.

“Content isn’t static — tone, emotion and subject matter often shift from one scene to the next. With scene-level analysis and real-time categorization, advertisers now have the ability to respond to that nuance and target accordingly. Scene-level signals introduce precision where it matters most — at the moment of exposure — and bring greater precision to how CTV campaigns are planned and evaluated.” Madrid said.

While more than half of marketers invest in TV and streaming, per Digiday Research, challenges remain in the CTV ad ecosystem. Though CTV investment continues to grow, advertisers still lack consistent, granular signals about content environments. And with the high impact CTV brings — delivering ads on the biggest screen in the home, often in non-skippable environments — context matters even more.

Many moments and scenes within a single TV program can be wildly different. As the show’s narrative progresses, the genre and emotional tone may also change, but program- or channel-level targeting doesn’t capture that nuance.

For instance, the popular series “Succession” was rooted in serious drama, but still delivered moments of sharp, often unexpected humor where an emotionally intense ad would feel out of place. Scene-level targeting aims to avoid that dissonant experience by using a more granular taxonomy to deliver more relevant, timely ads that enhance outcomes for both brands and audiences.

This additional layer of insight supports smarter creative alignment and stronger brand outcomes. According to a joint Wurl and TVision study, there is a 2.4x increase in attention when streaming TV ads match the content’s emotion.

Beyond helping to pinpoint the genre and emotional context, scene-level targeting is also well-suited for addressing brand safety concerns.

Typical channel- and show-level metadata doesn’t catch sensitive, inappropriate or generally misaligned content at the scene level. Because of this limited information, some advertisers entirely avoid potentially problematic content types, like news, to circumvent brand safety issues.

However, Wurl data shows that news accounts for an average of 8.6% of viewing hours on free ad-supported streaming TV, the majority of which is watched by a small but highly engaged segment of viewers — meaning that advertisers that exclude news content from their campaigns are missing out on opportunities to reach a sizable, engaged audience. Furthermore, Wurl’s 2026 CTV trends report found that more than a third of news scenes are fully brand safe. With scene-level targeting, ads can be placed alongside a feel-good news story, rather than a polarizing one, ensuring brand safety while still maximizing reach and message impact.

“Currently, many advertisers are approaching brand safety with overly broad-stroke approaches. Entire genres, programs and sometimes even publishers are excluded in an effort to avoid potential risk,” Madrid said. “By moving beyond broad proxies, advertisers can deliver their ads more intentionally, without sacrificing reach or risk to their brand. For publishers, this unlocks an optimization tool — shifting brand safety from a constraint into a monetization opportunity.”

For instance, Wurl uses generative AI to analyze video content, evaluating the scene immediately before an ad pod. This proprietary technology scores content across various attributes, including emotions, genres and IAB-defined brand safety categories.

“Our technology analyzes a combination of visual cues, audio signals and dialogue to understand imagery, tone, sentiment and contextual themes as they unfold within a scene,” Madrid said. “By evaluating the combination of these media aesthetics, we advance beyond one-dimensional metadata signals and produce dynamic segment outputs that classify the nuance of what’s happening in the moment. For advertisers, that means stronger alignment and attention. For publishers, it creates high-value, contextually enriched inventory.”

This analysis happens in real time, enabling dynamic and precise ad delivery at scale. As the content is identified and classified, these scores are appended to the content metadata before it is sent to demand-side platforms, where segments can then be targeted. Once these signals are passed into the bid stream, advertisers can choose to target relevant contextual moments that align with their brand.

“Brands have flexibility in how they activate contextual segments for their campaign targeting. They can select on their own from our expansive taxonomy, or more commonly, from our curated packages, where we use a proven combination of segment categories that perform well for specific advertiser verticals: food and beverage, travel, app installs, etc.,” Madrid said. “Regardless of whether advertisers run programmatic or managed service campaigns, they can choose from several options for how to best align their brand with content and the consumer’s mindset.”

Emotion is a powerful signal, and emotional tone changes from scene to scene. Because each scene carries a unique mood and message, real-time analysis ensures ads match the current moment and are aligned with viewers’ emotional state.

Emotions also play a significant role in decision-making and ad success. According to research from Integral Ad Science, ads aligned with the consumer’s emotional state or surrounding content are up to 40% more memorable. Other studies show that more than 90% of purchasing decisions are driven by emotional responses.

In practice, emotions are more complex than just happy or sad — and savvy advertisers use these subtleties to their advantage across the funnel.

Consider this lower-funnel example, in which global agency PMG leveraged Wurl’s scene-level targeting to run emotionally targeted CTV ads on behalf of a quick-service restaurant (QSR) client. Through scene-level targeting, the ads were matched to content that evoked feelings of anticipation and joy to drive measurable sales results, validated by independent measurement partner InMarket.

“By optimizing delivery around those high-performing emotional signals, such as anticipation, the campaign drove a 40% lift in incremental restaurant visits and a 48% lift in incremental sales, making emotion-led placements the top-performing CTV tactic in the flight,” Madrid said. “It’s a clear example of how moment-level alignment increases attention — and attention translates directly into measurable business impact.”

As Madrid explained, driving outcomes and measuring performance in CTV has been a top-of-mind challenge for advertisers. Third-party validation shows that when ads align with what’s on screen, advertisers see benefits such as improved brand recall, engagement and purchase intent.

“Through working with various measurement partners like Kantar, Upwave, EDO and InMarket, what we’re really seeing is that targeting at the scene level improves effectiveness holistically — not just at one stage, but throughout the entire customer journey,” Madrid said. “At the top, we see lifts in brand awareness and favorability. Mid-funnel, stronger motivation and purchase intent. And at the lower funnel, measurable outcomes like incremental store visits and sales.

“When creative aligns with mindset and moment, the ad feels less disruptive and more relevant — and that drives performance across the funnel because those moments directly influence attention and impact.”

Scene-level targeting does not need to be used in a vacuum. It can be easily added to advertisers’ existing strategies, helping them reach their target audience in a CTV context that is relevant to their campaign and creative.

For instance, in the QSR campaign mentioned previously, Wurl ran a controlled CTV test designed to evaluate two targeting approaches: audience and regional targeting alone and audience and regional targeting layered with scene-level targeting. The agency tapped an independent measurement firm to validate results and provide a clear view into the incremental impact of each strategy.

The campaign analysis revealed that adding scene-level targeting nearly doubled the performance of audience and regional targeting alone. By combining geographic, audience and scene-level targeting, the campaign effectively delivered both relevance and reach.

“When done correctly, combining CTV targeting strategies is where precision really starts to take shape,” Madrid said. “Audience and demographic targeting have long been the norm, ensuring the right viewers are being reached. Scene-level contextual targeting ensures they are being reached during the right moments of their viewing session — in this case, following content that is both brand-suitable and safe. When those layers work together, advertisers don’t have to choose between scale and suitability. It’s a ‘yes, and.’”

To fully unlock the potential of CTV advertising, marketers need to adapt their strategies to the streaming landscape. The good news: adding scene-level targeting to existing CTV toolkits doesn’t have to be a heavy lift.

The easiest way to begin is by layering scene-level signals on top of existing audience and demographic targeting — enhancing relevance without sacrificing scale. This allows marketers to maintain reach while improving the context and impact of their messaging.

When evaluating partners, it’s important to prioritize solutions that deliver both precision and scale. Look for providers that offer high-quality, real-time signals and can seamlessly integrate into existing programmatic workflows — enabling activation without added operational complexity. Signal accuracy and ease of activation are critical, as these inputs ultimately determine campaign performance.

As the ecosystem evolves, advertisers will have greater flexibility in how they access and activate contextual data. New approaches are making it easier to pair premium streaming inventory with differentiated contextual solutions, giving marketers more choice without disrupting existing buying workflows. For instance, Wurl’s Content Intelligence Platform reflects this shift, enabling advertisers to access Wurl’s own scene-level targeting, as well as helping partners like KERV, Peer39 and IRIS.TV to scale their own precise and unique CTV targeting solutions. For marketers, that means greater choice, more relevant contextual signals and the flexibility to activate the solutions that best align with their campaign objectives.

This flexibility will become even more important as programmatic buying becomes more automated and AI-driven. In an environment driven by agentic workflows and automated decision-making, campaign performance will depend on the quality of the underlying contextual data.

“We hear about agentic workflows and AI-driven solutions being championed across the industry — how it will improve efficiency, efficacy, creative and ultimately provide cost savings across organizations of all sizes. All of these promises translate into improved performance,” Madrid said. “As more investment flows into these agentic environments, we cannot ignore the fact that it’s the underlying data sources that underpin the success of automation and curation. Scene-level segments and real-time categorization support all of the above.”

In the wake of LiveRamp’s acquisition by Publicis, some marketers are auditioning alternatives to the identity graph company.

OpenAI has ambitious ad revenue targets, which depend on advertiser demand, measurement and monetisation.

Agencies and marketers now frequently use AI tools to create media and creative briefs. But the practice could be a canary in the coalmine for AI overuse in advertising.

#7339. Levi’s claymation holiday campaign celebrates handmade craft amid AI bloat

“Let’s Get Fuzzy” is centered on a 60-second spot that required four hours of filming for each second of animation.

Levi’s is taking inspiration from a 30-year-old campaign for its holiday effort. “Let’s Get Fuzzy” serves as a call back to the 1995 ad “Clayman,” the brand’s first global commercial and the first full-length spot completely shot in claymation, according to the brand. The brand’s decision to leverage claymation — which is considered to be incredibly labor intensive — could be welcomed by consumers, the majority of whom are growing tired of AI-generated content, particularly in advertising.

The 60-second video opens on a remote cabin in a snow-covered wilderness where friends are enjoying a holiday gathering. The night takes a turn as a ferocious yeti named Gus tries to break into the cabin before successfully running through the wall. Right when all hope seems to be lost, one of the characters throws a red sweater at the yeti in an apparent act of self defense. However, the sweater fits Gus perfectly, stopping his rampage and leading him to join the party as a welcomed guest.

The campaign, which launched two days after Halloween, meshes holiday aesthetics with vibes of the spooky season with its framing and soundtrack choice, akin to cult classics like “Dead Snow.” The video required four hours of filming for each second of animation, and the set and characters were made by hand, according to press details. No AI was used in the filming process, per the brand.

The collection is available via the brand’s website and app, as well as at select retail locations.

“Our Levi’s Let’s Get Fuzzy campaign was an opportunity to experiment with immersive and tactile world-building,” said Kenny Mitchell, global CMO at Levi’s, in press materials. “Anchored by a film created entirely with stop-motion claymation, it’s also the celebration of the craft and human creativity synonymous with the Levi’s brand.”

Generative AI has become an increasingly common tool for marketers as the industry looks to cut costs and meet the demands of an ever-evolving cultural landscape. However, cost savings and quick turnaround times come at a price. Consumers routinely find AI creative as “annoying,” “boring” and often “confusing,” according to a 2024 study by NielsenIQ. Half of consumers prefer brands that don’t use generative AI in advertising and other forward-facing messaging, per a 2026 Gartner study.

Levi’s isn’t the only company looking to use handcrafted creatives this holiday season. E.l.f. and Prada also launched handmade, stop-motion style holiday campaigns. Retail giants Walmart and Target both launched their campaigns before Halloween. The holiday shopping window has continued to get earlier as consumers hunt for the best value amid budgetary pressure.

Parent Levi Strauss & Co. reported a third-quarter revenue of $1.6 billion, a 4% year-over-year increase, according to an earnings release. Michelle Gass, CEO, president and director of Levi Strauss, noted on an earnings call that there has been less brand-building moments this year and that the brand’s back-to-school campaign did not generate the anticipated demand and traffic. The company plans to deepen its marketing investments and place more of a focus on mid-funnel marketing, according to Gass.

#7338. FTC Unseals Details In Amazon Ads Auction Surcharge Suit via @sejournal, @brookeosmundson

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

The FTC unsealed new details in its Amazon Ads lawsuit, alleging hidden surcharges raised CPCs by up to 250% on Prime Day. Here is what advertisers should know.

The Federal Trade Commission (FTC) and 22 state attorneys general filed an updated complaint against Amazon with newly unredacted details about how the company allegedly inflated prices in its online search advertising auction.

The FTC and its state partners originally sued Amazon in August in the Western District of Washington, alleging the retailer covertly raised the prices advertisers paid on its platforms.

The newly unsealed material alleges that Amazon secretly increased advertising prices by up to 50% above auction results on ordinary days. During major shopping events like Prime Day and Black Friday, the alleged increases reached 250%.

Read on to learn what the unredacted complaint alleges, how Amazon responded to advertiser questions at the time, and what this means for how you evaluate historical campaign performance.

According to the FTC’s November 5 announcement, the unsealed sections of the complaint outline specific cost-per-click (CPC) increases and revenue targets tied to the alleged surcharges between 2021 and 2023.

The complaint alleges that Amazon’s hidden surcharges affected both small businesses and large advertisers across multiple peak shopping periods:

These examples add detail to the original lawsuit, which challenged how Amazon calculated auction prices and explained those calculations to advertisers.

The unsealed material also links Amazon’s alleged surcharges to internal revenue goals.

According to the complaint, Amazon executives discussed needing an additional $40 million in revenue in 2023 to hit corporate financial targets.

The complaint quotes an email from the head of Amazon Ads to Amazon’s senior vice president of ad products and technology:

I’d like to pull out all of the stops (without doing unnatural acts) to close our gaps to plan. I suspect that the entire gap won’t be closed, so whatever can be done with reserves (ideally at least $40MM would be helpful).

The case concerns Amazon’s use of “soft reserve prices,” which can increase the final CPC above what competing bids would otherwise produce.

A soft reserve differs from a minimum bid required to enter an auction. Amazon says advertisers can win placements below the soft reserve, in which case they pay their bid.

The FTC alleges Amazon used undisclosed reserve pricing to increase advertiser charges while continuing to describe its auctions in misleading terms.

If you managed Amazon Ads between 2021 and 2023, you may remember questioning why CPCs jumped so sharply during peak shopping events.

According to the unredacted complaint, advertisers questioned Amazon directly about whether changes to its auction process explained higher CPCs.

In one instance, an advertiser asked whether “something has changed with respect to the second price auction process, either by design or mistake.”

The FTC alleges that an Amazon account manager gave a misleading response:

After reaching out to the product specialist team we were informed there is not a bug, or a change in auction style, so no worries there. This year the marketplace is even more crowded than any year previous, and we are seeing the spike in CPCs [costs-per-click] as a direct reflection.

The alleged exchange concerns more than whether CPCs increased. It concerns whether Amazon accurately explained the cause when an advertiser asked about changes to the auction.

Campaign reports show what advertisers paid and the performance that followed. They do not reveal how Amazon calculated the final CPC.

In its response to the original lawsuit, Amazon denied deceiving or harming advertisers. The company defended its reserve pricing and said advertisers never pay more than their maximum bid.

While these allegations are still being litigated in federal court, the unsealed details give paid media teams important context for past and current campaign analysis.

If you manage Amazon Ads for your brand or clients, here’s a few proactive steps you can take:

The 2021 through 2023 examples also shouldn’t be read as the full scope of the case. Earlier FTC materials discuss alleged conduct in 2024 and identify Sponsored Products, Sponsored Brands, and Display Ads.

The FTC’s press release summarizes the newly unredacted allegations, but it doesn’t state whether the alleged surcharge practices continued past 2023 or which specific Amazon search ad formats were included.

The newest release didn’t include a public response from Amazon regarding the newly unsealed details, however, Amazon did provide a full response dispute on August 31st regarding the lawsuit.

We’ll continue to report on this as more information from the court filings becomes available, or if Amazon provides an updated response to this new information.

#7337. Target vs. Walmart: Holiday marketing’s stakes feel higher this year

The big-box rivals are competing for price-sensitive shoppers at a moment when they are both trying to shift perceptions of their businesses in a broader sense.

The holidays are typically make-or-break for retailers on the sales front. For Target and Walmart, this season will also test efforts to refine and redefine their respective brand images as they compete for price-sensitive shoppers.

Walmart for the past year and a half has been building out a “Who Knew?” platform designed to shift consumer perceptions, highlighting a more on-trend assortment and better deals than people might expect. The concept comes to life for the holidays with a riff on “The Muppet Christmas Carol” starring Walton Goggins as Ebenzer Scrooge and a cast of Muppets including Kermit the Frog and Miss Piggy.

Target meanwhile is trying to sustain momentum for a turnaround strategy aimed at recapturing its “Tar-zhay” magic — a cheap-but-chic allure that was diluted during the bumpy post-pandemic years, when the brand got bogged down in culture wars backlash and lackluster store presentation. Performance has been on the upswing so far in 2026 following a CEO transition, but the holidays will be the real proving ground for Target, analysts say. Its campaign is anchored on a gifting academy for helping customers find the perfect present for different people in their life, with the institution led by a wise-cracking expert played by D’Arcy Carden.

“For Target, they’re really trying to reassert their difference,” said Taylre Stumpf, a retail consultant at Kantar who specializes in Target and mass channel retailers. “Walmart's is more of that nostalgia, that reappraisal: ‘You know us, but now see us differently.’”

The two campaigns carry similarities, chiefly in the form of storytelling that uses colorful characters and a fictional setting to tout features like product assortment and speedy delivery. The retailers in the past took a more fragmented approach to their holiday marketing to capitalize on specific seasonal moments like Black Friday, but a single narrative provides a greater sense of consistency for consumers. It could also be a way to differentiate in a category that has overskewed toward a deals and promotions focus amid persistent inflation.

“Rather than shouting price, they're shouting that storyline of, trust us, [we have] the best gifts,” said Stumpf.

Walmart’s “Who Knew?” is part of an evolution in the retailer’s strategy as it wins over higher-income shoppers and revamps segments like apparel. Miss Piggy will promote trendy fashion items for the holidays as part of “A Who Knew Carol,” as will content creators, Walmart CMO William White previously told Marketing Dive. The overall effect is that Walmart, in presentation and product focus, looks a bit more like Target, adding a new dimension to the competition between long-standing big-box rivals.

“The mass channel, which is Target and Walmart, they're both fighting for that same high income, still careful shopper, but using the same tools,” said Stumpf. “We just see value now as a type of table stakes, so both retailers now have to get creative on how to connect with each shopper.”

Walmart has over 4,600 brick-and-mortar stores in the U.S. while Target has fewer than half that. Walmart grew revenue 5.9% year over year to $187.9 billion in Q2 while U.S. comparable sales, an important measure of retailer health, were up 2.6% for the period. Target’s net sales were up 5.3% YoY to $26.5 billion in Q2 while comparable sales and traffic increased 3.8% and 3.6%, respectively. Target raised its full-year guidance around the Q2 earnings report in August.

“Walmart has definitely been giving Target a run for their money,” said Stumpf. “I think the last several years of Target underperforming, Walmart has taken advantage of that and has done very well.”

Target may be smaller than Walmart, but it has strengths in areas like personalization where it will need to double down to win in Q4, according to Stumpf. Target succeeds in connecting popular online items into an in-store presence and is tuned into the tastes of a specific type of millennial shopper, Stumpf explained. The “Holiday Academy of Giftology” campaign illustrates this approach through different departments and faculty members specializing in toys and gifts for people who claim they don’t want anything.

“It uses a construct of education and curriculum to come up with new ways to solve some of those typical holiday gifting problems that we all experience,” Target Chief Brand Officer Michelle Mesenburg said at a holiday preview event for press in October.

Consumers could be more selective during the holidays this year due to macroeconomic stresses. Outlooks for the holidays are mixed: While Deloitte’s consumer surveys point to spending on retail goods remaining robust, Kantar ShopperScape data shared by Stumpf indicates 16% of shoppers are planning to spend less this year.

“Target knows it can't outprice Walmart. If Walmart's already trying to take pages out of its playbook with the storytelling … all Target really has left is personalization and curation for its shoppers,” said Stumpf.

Another potential advantage for Target is in omnichannel. Visual elements of its academy-themed ads are reflected in physical retail stores and online. Target earlier this year overhauled its creator programs to be more well-rounded. Its holiday plans leverage Club Target, which is targeted at smaller and mid-sized creators on platforms like TikTok, as well as an exclusive partnership with LTK centered on bigger-name influencers.

“Target is really the only retailer I'm seeing right now that does a great job of blending the whole unified experience through their different tiers of creators,” said Stumpf.

#7335. Media Briefing: Publishers’ revenue growth defies traffic declines, but tougher comparisons loom

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Media Briefing covers the latest in media trends for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Thursday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

This week’s Media Briefing looks at how The New York Times, People Inc., News Corp and Reuters are navigating tougher year-over-year revenue comparisons after reporting strong growth despite declining referral traffic and AI disruption.

Declining referral traffic and AI disruption aren’t stopping some of the largest publishers from growing revenue, according to recent earnings. The New York Times, News Corp, People Inc., Reuters all reported gains in the third quarter, fueled by a mix of subscriptions, advertising and content licensing.

But their latest earnings also point to a common challenge heading into the final part of the year: sustaining that momentum as last year’s revenue gains make for tougher comparisons.

Notably, AI licensing has delivered real revenue growth for People Inc. and Reuters, especially as they look to diversify revenue beyond advertising. But the initial boost from those deals is becoming harder to replicate as those agreements mature.

“The short-term revenue win that publishers got by striking LLM deals helps for a quarter or four to eight, but it’s often at the expense of lost audience and inventory,” said Matt Prohaska, CEO and principal of Prohaska Consulting.

The New York Times is also bracing for tougher year-over-year comparisons in the fourth quarter, although in its case, the challenge stems from an advertising supply increase in 2025.

Meanwhile, News Corp’s latest earnings painted a more mixed picture, revealing publishers face different levels of exposure to declining audiences and traditional advertising pressures, even within the same company. The company posted strong revenue growth in its Dow Jones business, amid declines in its News Media division.

Digiday broke down what media executives said in their earnings call this week so far (still a few more to go, with USA Today Co., BuzzFeed and Ziff Davis later this week), and what it reveals about where publishers see their businesses heading next:

What was said: “The comps get tougher as the year goes on. Q4 is our biggest quarter in ads this year and last year. I’ll remind you we grew 25%. And last year we benefited from these sort of structural supply increases in certain parts of the portfolio.” – Meredith Kopit Levien, CEO, The New York Times Company.

What it really means: The Times benefited from a significant expansion of ad inventory last year, helping drive 25% YOY growth in digital advertising in Q4 2025. That sets a high baseline for the same quarter this year, making it more difficult to sustain that pace of growth, Kopit Levien said in the earnings call on Nov. 4. Digital advertising revenue growth has already slowed, from 20.7% YOY in Q2 2026 to 12% in Q3.

Because of this, the Times expects more modest advertising growth this quarter – though Kopit Levien said in the earnings call that the company will continue to add supply going forward, though it will be “more incremental and more across the portfolio.”

What was said: “The licensing revenue, as I said and as we saw, was really impressive. We continue to have real success across Apple News, AI, and other content partnerships. In Q4, those growth rates will slow some as we lap the Meta AI deal that we announced last Q4. We still expect full-year growth to be about what we delivered in licensing in the first half which translates to something roughly about 20% Q4 growth. Again, our focus is to get back to the growth we want and expect.” – Tim Quinn, CFO, People Inc.

What it really means: People Inc. has three publicly disclosed AI licensing deals, with OpenAI, Meta and Microsoft. The Meta deal Quinn referenced during the Nov. 3 earnings call was signed last December. While People Inc.’s licensing revenue grew 49% year over year in Q3 2026, Quinn cautioned investors against expecting a similar growth rate in the fourth quarter, as the company begins to lap the initial revenue boost from its Meta deal.

But People Inc. execs remained optimistic about the AI licensing market. “They will continue to evolve and there’s a big place for our content. There’s increasingly a place for recency — meaning new content — in that marketplace,” Quinn said in the earnings call.

However, the majority of People Inc.’s licensing revenue growth came from non-AI content licensing deals. Apple News has been a “great success,” Quinn said, alongside distribution deals with platforms such as Yahoo and MSN. In total, about 15 of People Inc.’s brands are successfully generating content licensing revenue, Quinn added. Overall, People Inc.’s digital revenue grew 2% YOY in Q3 2026.

What was said: “We expect Reuters growth to be flattish year-over-year in the fourth quarter due to lapping $5 million of transactional AI licensing revenue from the prior year period, along with tougher comps at agency and in portions of the professional business.”  – Gary Bischoping, CFO, Thomson Reuters.

What it really means: Reuters also has three publicly disclosed AI content licensing deals: with Meta and Microsoft in October 2024, and a deal with Amazon in May 2026. However, a Reuters executive previously told Digiday that the company has other AI content licensing deals that it has not spoken about publicly, and cited the company’s earnings as a sign of this. The full list of Reuters’ AI licensing partners and the number of deals is not clear. But what is clear is that revenue from these deals has helped boost Reuters’ overall business.

Reuters organic revenue grew 12% in Q3 2026, thanks to about $19 million of transactional generative AI content licensing in the quarter, per Bischoping. When excluding transactional AI licensing revenue in the third quarter of both 2026 and 2025, Reuters’ revenue grew 7% YOY. While AI licensing was a meaningful contributor to Reuters’ growth this past quarter, it can create volatility in year-over-year comparisons, as its not recurring revenue for the company.

Also, stronger prior-year performance in Reuters’ agency and professional businesses will mean tougher comps in Q4 2026, Bischoping said.

What was said: “News Media continues to navigate complex market pressures, including a challenging advertising environment and the ongoing evolution of the search and social algorithm landscape. As mentioned earlier, that search landscape will change dramatically with the advent of agentic AI. Revenues for the segment declined slightly versus prior year to $540 million.” – Robert Thomson, CEO, News Corp

What it really means: Revenue at News Corp’s Dow Jones business — which includes titles like The Wall Street Journal, Barron’s and MarketWatch — grew 8% year over year, fueled by subscriptions, licensing and its professional information businesses, according to its earnings released on Nov. 4. Digital advertising revenue increased 12%, matching the growth rate reported by The New York Times in Q3. These results are an example of how large publishers are finding ways to grow revenue beyond traditional traffic-dependent business models, even as some face tougher year-over-year comparisons ahead.

However, revenue at the company’s News Media division — which includes The Sun, The Times of London and the New York Post — declined 1% YOY. The division’s ad revenue fell 2% YOY, despite gains in subscription pricing and content licensing revenue. Audience declines were also evident: The Sun’s global monthly unique users fell 26% YOY in September, while the New York Post’s digital audience declined 11%.

“I think this is useless and is marketing, and far, far, far too late… It does nothing and doesn’t solve the problem at all. And so it’s purely window dressing.”

– Paul Bannister, chief strategy officer at Raptive, on the robots.txt “policy token” multiple AI companies have agreed to honor, which they say will allow publishers to opt out of having their content used to train generative AI models.

15%: The YOY growth of People Inc.’s non-session-based revenue in Q3 2026, due to strength in licensing, content syndication, events and social video. This now makes up 43% of People Inc.’s overall digital revenue.

26%: The reduction in unique visitors to participating Digital Content Next members’ sites from 2023 to 2025, according to data collected by the publisher trade group and included in an amicus brief filed in a copyright lawsuit against OpenAI and Microsoft.

38%: The share of people using AI to find information weekly, up from 11% in 2024, making it the main use of AI, according to the Reuters Institute for the Study of Journalism’s “Generative AI and News Report 2026.”

51%: The share of people using AI tools weekly in 2026, nearly triple the 18% reported in 2024, according to the Reuters Institute report.

Publishers furious over Meta’s AI scraping terms still can’t quit Facebook

Political creators juggle platforms to balance reach, revenue and unpredictable rules

People Inc. builds custom APIs to make its journalism easier for AI agents to access

Giggly Squad’s $160 million SiriusXM deal signals a shift from podcast networks to creator ecosystems

OpenAI, Google and Microsoft back new standard for publishers to block AI scraping

OpenAI, Google, Microsoft and Anthropic have agreed to support a new robots.txt “policy token” standard that would allow publishers to opt out of their content being scraped and used for AI training, without needing to configure restrictions for each one individually, The Verge reported.

Condé Nast faces $35 million ad revenue shortfall

Condé Nast is reportedly on track to miss its Q4 U.S. advertising target by more than $35 million, amid senior sales departures and uncertainty over its commercial strategy, Adweek reported.

People Inc. chief executive Neil Vogel told staff in a memo that the company will cut 195 people, as a result of the “rapid changes in media,” The New York Times reported.

New York Times and other publishers sue Brave over AI scraping

The New York Times and several other newspapers (including the Daily News, the Chicago Tribune, and the Orlando Sentinel) have sued Brave, accusing the web browser company of illegally scraping their content for AI platforms and allowing users to bypass their paywalls, Reuters reported.

Bloomberg to pay $500,000 over misleading subscription practices

DoorDash’s announced plans to acquire Instacart for $17 billion point to the constant need to gain market share by diversifying services and offerings.

Publishers are pushing back against Meta’s AI scraping terms, testing their leverage over a platform they still rely on.

Political and news creators are relied on now more than ever, but what platforms are working for them? Which ones aren’t?

#7334. How Fresh Step used AI to go from brief to ads at one-tenth the cost

The Clorox cat litter brand worked with agency RECE to get a new campaign in market in about a month.

Since its launch in 1984, Fresh Step has been the crown jewel of Clorox’s cat litter portfolio, a segment of the CPG marketer’s business that has lagged for some time and does not command the share of the $5 billion U.S. cat litter market that executives would like. The company determined that part of the brand’s problem was heightened category pressure on its core promise around odor control.

“Perceptions of [Fresh Step] had actually started to dip. Not core perceptions — it was still good — but relative perceptions,” Eric Schwartz, CMO of The Clorox Company, said about Fresh Step during a presentation at the Association of National Advertisers Masters of Marketing conference last month.

To address those issues and reassert Fresh Step’s positioning as being superior in odor control, the brand needed a new campaign that would be memorable and engaging, but made quickly. The marketer wanted to be able to pivot easily, if needed, rather than be stuck after betting the farm on a creative concept.

“There's a real business pressure, and it was not going to be acceptable [to say] I'll come in six months with my idea,” Deb Crandall, senior marketing director on litter at The Clorox Company, said in an interview. “We need things in market ASAP.”

Fresh Step teamed with artificial intelligence-powered agency RECE — short for radical empathy and creative excellence — to go from brief to finished ad creative on three 15-second spots in 30 days and at one-tenth the cost of a traditional production (and without having to rely on finicky cat actors).

“The speed AI creates lets us bring strategy, concept, copy, direction, AI artists, editors and composers together, all at once. It’s closer to a tech startup’s prototyping process than a traditional ad agency’s,” Toygar Bazarkaya, founder and chief creative officer at RECE, said in emailed comments.

The ads are rolling out now across linear TV with cutdowns on social and will be live by Nov. 9.

“This campaign could not have existed in this way, probably even a year ago. That's how fast I think the advances [in AI] are coming,” Crandall said. “The technology unlocked some magic that wouldn't have been possible without it.”

Fresh Step’s new ads feature a cast of talking cats of different breeds with different accents and attitudes. The spots deliver the brand’s value proposition around odor in different ways: a dog can no longer smell its way to a litter box; a huge cat is no problem for the brand; and cats line up around the block to use Fresh Step.

The brief began as usual, with a business challenge and a consumer insight. AI helped to rapidly prototype creative concepts to show what was resonating, delivering emotion in a way that a script on the page can’t always do. Being able to see talking cats in action helped convince the brand that the ads would still connect with consumers.

“One of the fears was would AI cats give you that same emotive response as when you see a real animal? I think the work does deliver on that. It captures that emotional response,” Crandall said. “That inner part of the process — being able to see it and feel it like midstream — really helped us to unlock and push us into some new spaces that we hadn't been before as a brand.”

The fact that AI-powered content creation technology is trained on an internet that is still overloaded with cat videos may make it easier for the AI-created talking cats to cross the uncanny valley than AI-created humans. And while consumers are wary of AI in ads, the Fresh Step ads are clearly computer generated.

“As consumers, we're all experimenting with this technology in our lives and figuring out how it helps us and doesn't help us,” Crandall said. “This, to me, was very clearly AI. We're not trying to fool anybody here. We're using it to dramatize situations that many cat owners can relate to.”

The speed and cost efficiency of the AI-created campaign will help Fresh Step reinvest in the brand: Getting it done at one-tenth the cost roughly equates to being able to create 10 more campaigns.

“It's always a sign when you've hooked onto something when you can already think of the next five ideas that you want to go do. We have some other things that we're thinking about creating once we get these out there and see how they resonate,” Crandall said. “It's that beautiful blend of humor and connecting to real cat-parent truths that I think is the secret sauce.”

Plus, the creativity unlocked by technology is helping the brand to extend the campaign into what CMO Schwartz referred to as systems of relevance that continuously deliver organic content.

“The deliberately modular design lets us adapt benefits, innovations, packaging, even messages based on what we will learn about consumers in market,” the executive said of the campaign at the ANA event. “That's the real advantage. This isn't a piece of creative we had to bet big on, and now we let it fly and leave it alone. We can keep learning, refining and improving as we go.”

#7333. Senior ML Engineer - Malware Detection (remote work) at CloudLinux

CloudLinux is a global remote-first company. We are driven by our principles: do the right thing, employees first, we are remote first, and we deliver high-volume, low-cost Linux infrastructure and security products that help companies to increase the efficiency of their operations. Every person on our team supports each other and does what we can to ensure we all are successful.

Check out our website for more information: https://cloudlinux.com/

Imunify360 is CloudLinux's security suite for Linux web servers: real-time and background malware scanning, automated cleanup, a WAF, runtime PHP protection (Proactive Defense), a database malware scanner, and IP reputation. It runs on hosting providers' shared, VPS, and dedicated servers, so new web-malware campaigns land on our fleet early.

We sit on one of the largest labeled corpora of live web-server malware in the industry: files, verdicts, cleanup outcomes, and analyst decisions collected from production hosting servers every day. It already drives our signatures, heuristics, and ML models, and there is far more to get out of it. We are looking for a Senior ML Engineer who will turn that data into detection that ships — classifiers, LLM-assisted triage and generated signatures — and keep exploring new ML/LLM methods against it, measured by the product's false-negative and false-positive rates, not by notebook accuracy.

Our dataset focuses on web malware — PHP, JavaScript, HTML phishing kits, infected WordPress and Joomla plugins and themes, database injections — with the context around each file.

The goal is 10x more malware detected at the same or lower false-positive rate. Hand-written rules and faster pipelines will not get there; models trained on our data might.

You get an experimentation track, GPUs, and a direct line to leadership; in return, you bring numbers, not opinions.

You own the path from raw samples to a model in production, and the numbers that prove it helped.

New remote jobs delivered to your inbox, every week.

Location independence is the future of work. It serves companies with best talent from around the globe. And benefits employees with a greater work life balance and the ability to work in their pyjamas.- Nithur, founder.

#7331. How marketers can better optimize for AI search visibility

Most of what marketers are expected to spend on artificial intelligence visibility in 2027 is misdirected, writes GeoSurge founder Francisco Vigo.

The following is a guest piece written by Francisco Vigo, co-founder and CEO of AI visibility company GeoSurge. Opinions are the author’s own.

Most of what marketers will spend on artificial intelligence visibility in 2027 is aimed at the wrong end of the process. The standard playbook tunes content for the moment that an assistant searches the web, assuming live search gives every brand a fair shot at being mentioned in AI results. By the time the model searches, though, it has already decided which brands are worth looking up, and it makes that call from memory.

We measured this at GeoSurge. Brands an AI model already remembered were searched about three times as often as brands it didn't, 55.7% versus 17.4%, and for its five most-remembered brands, the rate reached 67%. The pattern held in all nine industries we tested. If memory decides who gets searched, the budget belongs in what builds memory: the coverage, reviews and category association that models learn from between training runs.

When AI assistants first started answering buying questions, brands worried that the model's knowledge was frozen at training time. Live web search seemed to solve that, because whatever the model doesn't know, it can look up in real time. We call that RAG - Retrieval-Augmented Generation. However, before a model can retrieve anything, it has to decide what to search for, and it makes that decision using its own memory, somewhat like a human would.

Modern AI search doesn't run a single query. Ask "what's the best business checking account for a small company" and the model splits it into a batch of parallel searches. Some are generic and some name specific companies. Google calls this “query fan-out.” Every citation and recommendation flows through those queries, and a source that never matches one rarely enters the answer.

To see how memory shapes those queries, we first recorded the brands a model could recall with no web access, across nine industries. Then, over 12 days, we ran 66 realistic buyer questions 60 times each, capturing about 4,000 responses and 13,281 of the searches the model ran behind the scenes. About 69% of those searches were generic category queries. Of the 31% that named a brand, nearly two-thirds named one of the model's top-five remembered brands.

This makes sense mechanically. A model writing search queries draws on the same learned associations it uses to write anything else. It can't search for a brand it never learned, any more than you can Google a restaurant you've never heard of.

The effect isn't uniform, and that matters for marketers who are planning.

For example, in automotive, 82% of brand-specific searches named one of the model's top-five remembered brands; in finance, 77% did. In fitness and wellness, the figure was closer to 50%, because models searched beyond their memory far more often.

Our tentative read, which the study didn't test, is that memory-led categories are dominated by a few long-established, heavily covered brands. Search-led categories, by contrast, have many smaller, newer ones. Each industry rests on six to 12 prompts, so treat the splits as indicative. Still, the distinction is useful. It tells you whether your problem is retrievability, which content and technical work can fix relatively quickly, or memorability, which it can't.

First, diagnose before you spend. In a search-led category, well-structured and highly retrievable content can move results relatively quickly. In a memory-led category, the same work has a much lower ceiling.

Second, fund earned media as infrastructure. Model memory forms from the training corpus: years of press coverage, analyst reports, reviews, forum discussions and consistent association between a brand and its category. These disciplines were long justified in the soft language of “reputation.” Now they have a mechanical function, because they are how a brand gets written into the systems that decide what to search for. They can't be injected at query time; they accrue between training runs, or not at all.

Third, measure recall, not just citations. Counting citations measures the scoreboard, but the game is decided upstream, in what models remember before a single query fires. That scoreboard is also getting cheaper. Tracking that typically costs hundreds of dollars a month now has free options. If seeing the answer costs next to nothing, more of the budget can go toward shaping it.

None of this makes query-time optimization worthless. It works in search-led categories, and even in memory-led ones the generic queries leave room to compete.

But the industry has the priority order backward. The dominant playbook optimizes the last millisecond of a process that is substantially decided months earlier, inside a training run.

For challenger brands, this is urgent. Remembered brands get searched, searched brands get cited and those citations feed the corpus the next generation of models learns from. Each training cycle dominated by incumbents makes that loop harder to break.

You can't win at query time what you lost at training time; however, training time comes around again, and what the models remember next is being written now.

#7330. After DoorDash’s Instacart bid, here’s the case for and against consolidation in commerce media

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The “C” word (consolidation) might soon be coming for commerce media.

At least that was how media buyers and industry analysts reacted to last week’s news about DoorDash’s plans to acquire Instacart.

“We’ve seen this play out before in search, social, and streaming. Markets tend to consolidate around a few major players, and commerce media is heading in the same direction,” said Bill Wise, CEO and co-founder of Mediaocean, an ad tech company.

Should the deal, which is valued at approximately $17 billion, go through, it pairs DoorDash’s delivery network of 993 million total orders, per DoorDash’s Q3 earnings and Instacart’s grocery and fulfillment technology (as well as its 91.4 million orders, per Instacart’s Q3 earnings).

Here’s the case for and against consolidation in commerce media.

Perhaps the biggest case in favor of consolidation in commerce media is that the playbook already exists in ad tech. For example, Gopuff announced plans in September to leverage Instacart’s white-label retail media platform Carrot Ads. Both Gopuff and Instacart have separate commerce media networks.

“You could kind of argue data consolidation is also happening across partners who are working together to make their joint offerings more compelling,” said Briana Finelli, vp of brand and commerce media at Goodway Group.

As for the DoorDash-Instacart deal specifically, the upsides are a broader audience, more powerful closed-loop reporting capabilities, combined tech offerings and a more simplified media buying experience (buying with a single combined platform as opposed to across two individuals).

“Add in a scaled ads platform featuring sponsored products, search, offsite capabilities, social and search and what don’t you have,” said Elizabeth Marsten, vp of commerce innovation and growth at Tinuiti.

By 2028, eMarketer forecasts Amazon’s retail media revenues will rake in at least $75 billion. Advertisers are spending more on commerce and retail media, but are looking for simplicity in a fragmented marketplace.

“The opportunity is there to build a much more competitive retail media offering, but execution and speed will be critical,” said Nik Hengel, vp of analytics at New Engen digital marketing agency.

While the idea of bigger, farther reaching commerce media platforms sounds appetizing, consolidation doesn’t come without its considerations.

For starters, a shinier offering could come with a premium price tag. Or as Chief Media CEO Scott Paternoster puts it, “The cons could be everything that everyone thinks about consolidation, with price pressures and whatnot.”

There’s also the painful process of combining two separate companies’ culture, tech stacks, and staff that could disrupt how advertisers buy media. Already, advertisers have started to question what the DoorDash-Instacart merger means for capabilities and measurement.

Expect other questions around audience duplication, said Greg Carlucci, senior director analyst at Gartner.

While consolidation has its perks, one particular consideration is how much control advertisers have in negotiating with a behemoth. Just look at the relationship between indie agencies and major demand side platforms.

“That’s why independent infrastructure matters even more as the market consolidates,” said Wise. He added, “Advertisers need a consistent way to buy across platforms, with the right context, without being locked into any one ecosystem.”

OpenAI has ambitious ad revenue targets, which depend on advertiser demand, measurement and monetisation.

#7328. Reddit tests ads in search results

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The platform wants to help advertisers align their promotions with users’ discovery behavior even as the company’s overall search traffic drops.

Reddit is testing ads in search results in the app, as the platform looks for more ways to help advertisers align with discovery behaviors.

As shown in these example screens, Reddit’s search ads will be targeted by query, and will include a small ad marker in the upper section of the panel.

As explained by Reddit: “People come to Reddit seeking human perspectives and experiences they can’t find elsewhere, with half of Reddit users saying the platform helps them discover something new. As we continue improving the user search experience on Reddit, we’re also beginning to test ad placements in search. These placements leverage existing ad targeting in search results pages on Reddit, which have high purchase and search intent.”

That could reduce the value of its own in-app discovery process, though Tuesday’s announcement suggests that Reddit is clearly hoping to maintain its value as a product discovery tool.

Reddit said that it will initially test ads in search with a select group of eligible advertisers.

#7327. Agencies find AI overuse is leading to brief bloat

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is part of Digiday’s coverage of its Digiday Media Buying Summit. More from the series →

The advertising world rushed to embrace generative AI. But now that agencies and advertisers have come to rely on the tech to keep up, they’re finding themselves vulnerable to its toxic byproducts.

In particular, agency leaders are finding the over-use of AI tools to write advertising briefs risks undermining the productivity and the day-to-day sanity of marketing staffers.

According to M&C Saatchi’s North American CEO, Nadja Bellan-White, over-reliance on AI for brief writing is approaching “a tipping point… It’s a little bit ridiculous.” Speaking during last week’s Digiday Media Buying Summit, she estimated that 85% of the briefs sent to the agency by clients were written using AI tools.

The impact? Corporate documents that run into the hundreds of pages, replete with marketing jargon but little actual vision. “How many many times can you say ‘framework’ in one sentence? How many times can you try ‘economies of scale’? How many times can you tell me I need this to ‘tie back to commercial objectives’?”

Brief writing has become one of the most common applications for AI tech in marketing. More than half (56.4%) of marketers and agencies now use it to build out strategy, media or campaign creative briefs, according to a survey conducted by Basis Technologies between July and August.

Media agencies use agents to suggest audience segments, run analyses of competitors’ media plans or suggest search keywords to bid on. Creative shops and influencer agencies use AI to skip through reams of consumer research, brand guidelines and economic trends, or to find ways of trumping received marketing wisdom. In-house teams at high-spending brands use generative tools to spin up strategy approaches. Even publishers now use agentic tools that respond to client briefs with AI-generated insights.

Staffers will point to the hours clawed back or to the insights discovered from a broader or deeper reading of research. Behind closed doors, however, they kvetch about the cost.

“I’ve seen some of the worst briefs I’ve ever seen in my career recently, and it’s because they’re so disjointed and there’s no through-line on what… the marketing goal is,” said one agency executive, speaking under Chatham House rules at a townhall session at DMBS.

“AI is making it worse. It’s pontificating on a sentence structure that makes [a brief] 10 pages long,” said another.

Complaints about the proliferation of memos or emails, or indeed badly written campaign briefs, aren’t new. At the start of the decade, industry veterans will recall there was even a cross-industry initiative to encourage better brief writing. But just as it’s sped up the production of digital creative assets, Claude and ChatGPT tools have enabled bad writing to spread like knotweed. As another agency exec put it: “There’s a lack of critical thinking.”

Industry leaders are beginning to acknowledge those problems in public. “[AI] can be a good sparring partner,” said Bellan-White. “But at the end of the day, you need to be the one to make the decision. You understand the client’s briefs. You understand the market context. You’ve got to really think about what it is that’s recommended.”

It’s not hard to see how AI has become a crutch. Good briefs take time to craft, time that clients are loath to grant their agencies, or to use themselves, for something so far removed from the end product. Little surprise, then, that staffers turn to a solution that does the work for them like a white-collar version of the shoemaker’s elves.

In the Brothers Grimm tale, however, the shoemaker eventually retakes control of production. But advertising staffers are long-term practitioners of what academics call “cognitive offloading,” the delegation of knowledge work to a tool or machine.

A growing body of research suggests that such over-reliance on AI tools literally reduces the cognitive abilities of users. One 2025 MIT Media Lab study, for example, measured the brainwaves of students writing essays without the aid of technology, compared with those writing with the assistance of search engines or ChatGPT. The AI-assisted group showed a 55% reduction in brainwave activity.

Whatever the name, the signs are the same. A profusion of em-dashes, paragraph upon paragraph of empty, over-long sentences, the phrase “it’s not X, it’s Y.”

Cognitive offloading isn’t just an abstract (or aesthetic) problem for ad agency leaders, some of whom are recognizing that some marketing staffers have gone from using AI as an aid to relying upon it to prop them up. Bloated documents steal time from colleagues or clients, while many users’ loose attitude to the statistics AI tools conjure up demand for a closer-than usual supervision by fact-checkers. According to a GWI survey of 150 British marketers published last month, 29% admitted inaccurate or misleading insights had cost their companies financially.

“They have exported all of their thinking to AI,” said Tony Russo, vp, innovation and strategy at indie agency Mile Marker, during another DMBS session. “It’s a judgment problem.”

If there is a solution, it’s an offline one. Christina Riccitelli, chief innovation and analytics officer at media agency OpAD, said managers should intervene if they found junior staff leaning too hard on AI. “You have to reel them back in,” she said.

Bellan-White recommended more drastic action. “Turn off your laptop. Just literally shut it down. Turn off your phones,” she said. “I want you to look me in the eye and actually have a conversation. Do you know how uncomfortable that is for people today? Because if you actually have to have a conversation, that means you actually have to know what you’re talking about.”

OpenAI has ambitious ad revenue targets, which depend on advertiser demand, measurement and monetisation.

The DoorDash-Instacart deal could accelerate commerce media consolidation, promising scale but risking higher prices and lock-in.

As creator marketing becomes a fixture in many brands’ marketing strategies, creators’ roles are shifting and expanding.

#7324. In graphic detail: Six thousand brands later, ChatGPT ads still have a conversion problem

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

ChatGPT now handles roughly a fifth of the search queries Google does. Its ads business is worth considerably less. Nobody should be surprised by that. OpenAI only started selling ads this year, the format they’re growing into is still taking shape and the company has been tiptoeing around how much advertising to put in front of users. Advertising is, in other words, lagging usage. OpenAI will want that to change quickly and advertisers are already testing how fast it can.

There’s room for OpenAI to increase the reach of its ad business within markets where it already operates. According to data from Similarweb and Graphite, the U.K. has the highest proportion of ad-eligible ChatGPT users seeing ads (45.7%), followed by the U.S. (43.4%) and Australia (43.3%).

But the data shows fewer than half of eligible users, even in OpenAI’s most established ad markets, are currently being reached by ads. In Mexico, for example, that figure falls to just 27.8%. The differences could reflect how far along each market is in the advertising rollout, or how much of its eligible audience OpenAI is currently monetizing.

The mix of advertisers spending on ChatGPT is also broadening beyond retail. According to data from Sensor Tower, shopping accounted for 38% of U.S. ad spend on the platform in April, but that share fell to 20% by September.

Over the same period, financial services increased its share of spend on ChatGPT from 2% to 13%, while software grew from 13% to 18%, and travel and tourism increased from 5% to 9%. This suggests that ChatGPT is attracting demand from a wider range of advertisers, rather than relying on a narrow range of verticals looking to reach consumers in shopping-related moments.

This has certainly broadened since the ad pilot launched in February. While Sensor Tower only started tracking ad spend in April 2026, at launch, the company estimates that around 42% of ads on ChatGPT were from advertisers in the shopping, followed by jobs & education (10%), food & dining (9%), software (8%), media and entertainment (8%) and travel (7%).

The number of brands advertising on ChatGPT is growing too. Data from Adthena shows that it tracked 1,082 distinct brands running ads on the platform, across the U.S., Australia, the U.K. and the EU in June. That number rose to 3,741 in July and 6,333 in August. And while the data for September only goes as far as Sept. 20, that full month is expected to reach around 6,830, based on Adthena’s data and projections.

It points to a growing pool of brands advertising on ChatGPT as the platform expands its markets and advertising capabilities.

New Street Research models the number of commercial chats on ChatGPT to grow from 45.9 billion this year, to nearly 380 billion by 2030, as the proportion of total chats carrying ads increases from 20% to 28% during the same period.

But the volume of ad opportunities is only part of the equation. The way those conversations are monetized will also matter. OpenAI recently said CPC and outcome-optimized bidding account for the majority of its ad campaigns, a higher proportion than New Street had previously assumed in its own model.

That could be positive for revenue, given CPC ads typically generate more per-ad event than impression-based pricing. However, New Street cautions that monetization may currently be weaker than it had originally modelled, suggesting its estimated CPC of around $5 could be too high.

That said, there is a question over whether ChatGPT ads are currently delivering enough value to advertisers to support the kind of revenue growth OpenAI is targeting. Research from a working paper under review by Maximilian Kaiser and Christian Schulze, based on data from ecommerce websites, found that paid ChatGPT visits generate $0.89 in revenue per session for U.S. advertisers, compared with $2.45 for paid Google Search visits.

The difference was also reflected in conversion rates: 0.37% for paid ChatGPT traffic, compared with 1.17% for Google. The findings suggest that while advertisers are increasingly testing the platform, OpenAI still has work to do to demonstrate that its ads can compete with established performance channels.

Those performance questions are compounded by ongoing measurement issues, according to Accuracast’s group CEO Farhad Divecha, who said the latest partner integrations — Magnite and Pubmatic — have not necessarily addressed the underlying tracking issues.

“We continue to see discrepancies there,” he said. “Some are teething problems because it’s a new system that has to be set up on advertiser systems, but some problems are down to attribution being messed up.”

Agencies and marketers now frequently use AI tools to create media and creative briefs. But the practice could be a canary in the coalmine for AI overuse in advertising.

#7321. Why Liquid Death’s media chief is determined to break away from LiveRamp

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Liquid Death’s chief media officer could do without another branded hoodie this holiday season. A credible alternative to LiveRamp would go down far better.

Benoit Vatere has been looking for one since early August, when the beverage brand stopped the identity graph, after about 18 months of usage. The decision came weeks after Publicis Groupe’s plan to acquire LiveRamp was made public. In an interview with Digiday, Vatere said it was a simple choice: “I don’t want to train or have them see that super valuable information [for] anybody that I’m trying to get market share from.”

That fear stems from what LiveRamp learned about Liquid Death’s customers. The company’s job was to recognize the same people as they moved between screens and sites, so someone who saw an ad on a smart TV could be found again on a phone or a retailer’s site.

Over time that showed the brand which ads got people buying. Names and email addresses that get swept up in that tracking don’t concern Vatere since Publicis won’t see personal information. He’s far more concerned about the buying patterns. Publicis, after all, also works for brands Liquid Death competes with, like Evian and PepsiCo.

Vatere worries his patterns could end up in the data the holdco uses to train its AI tools and subsequently help make strategic calls for those rivals. Granted, the deal’s terms say that can’t happen. But Vatere expects them to change — terms and conditions get rewritten all the time, after all — not to mention the FTC’s watch over the deal lasts only five years.

“Let’s be clear about why Publicis bought LiveRamp,” continued Vatere. “They did it because there is value right? And the value is not just the revenue.”

That’s an indictment of every holdco with designs on an identity graph as it is of Publicis specifically. Vatere won’t hand his data to any company that also advises his competitors, which rules out the likes of Havas (still a LiveRamp user), WPP or Omnicom (which is building its own alternative).

In fact, Omnicom’s Acxiom has already tried its luck. Vatere sent it packing on the same grounds. “I’m not mad at Publicis. I just don’t want any of the holdcos to own it.”

That leaves independent providers, and Vatere isn’t naive about them either. Most are built with an acquisition and an exit in mind, so whichever one he picks will have to sign a contract that ends the relationship and deletes Liquid Death’s data the moment control changes hands.

“There should be a clause [that] if there’s change of ownership, change of control, contract out, right? Data deleted, like the whole thing, “ added Vatere.

He has a deadline of sorts. The LiveRamp contract runs out at the end of the year, and Liquid Death isn’t big enough to build its own graph. “I cannot do without it right now,” he said.

Chances are he won’t have to. The industry has no shortage of would-be LiveRamp replacements and plenty of them have already found their way to Vatere’s DMs. Earlier this week, the marketer posted his stance on LinkedIn after seeing reports that the holdco wasn’t concerned about competitors leaving now the deal has closed.

The replies read like a who’s who of the opportunists circling the fallout. One of them was LiveRamp’s CEO, said Vatere. He sent him a connection request, which was duly accepted. Nothing has come of it so far. Not much could. The only thing that would win him back is LiveRamp pulling out of the deal — and that ship has sailed.

Vatere is far from the only industry player to have taken this stance. LiveRamp rivals have been fielding calls and pitching for business since the deal was announced in the Spring. Both WPP and Omnicom have stopped working with the solution.

Advertisers have spent years investing heavily in cloud infrastructure, customer data platforms and first-party data infrastructure with the aim of retaining ownership of customer information as they activate media strategies. CMOs worried about maintaining that control may be at the front of the queue to speak to alternative providers.

“Advertising is an inherently zero-sum game. You need to beat your competitors to grow,” said Ruben Schreurs, CEO at Ebiquity. “Taking a uniform commoditized approach to communications or media is not a good strategy.”

“If your identity graph and your customer intelligence is your most important intellectual property, then you can’t choose infrastructure whose business model creates the appearance of conflicting incentives,” said Lai.

Schreurs argued, however, that working with LiveRamp alongside rivals won’t be the act that jeopardises a brand’s edge. “I do think it’s a little overplayed,” he said. “You can absolutely manage things in a way that if [your] agency also works for competing brands, that doesn’t necessarily have to be a problem. The same is true with LiveRamp.”

Whether any of this has a material impact on the trajectory of the business under Publicis remains uncertain. The likely answer is that Publicis wants LiveRamp’s data and connections for its own AI ambitions, combining LiveRamp’s clean room and data connectivity tech with the identity capabilities of Epsilon and the tech services of Publicis Sapient.

In any case, many of Vatere’s peers may find themselves boxed into to signing up with a Publicis-backed LiveRamp, or one of the rival holdco solutions, due to the market’s contraction in recent years.

“Clients absolutely care about conflict, how conflict is managed, but they have less and less optionality,” noted Schreurs. “There is now a big global three to choose from, so you’re going to work with a company that also works for competing brands. Then it becomes all about how you retain your competitive edge.”

Competitors and non-Publicis businesses are also a sliver of the business, accounting for roughly 5% of LiveRamp’s $800 million annual revenue.

A few defections at the edges are a price the holdco can afford to pay. Whether marketers like Vatere peel away in big enough numbers to change that sum remains an open question.

Now that Publicis has closed its acquisition of LiveRamp, marketers have a decision to make.

Google’s Q3 earnings reveal AI Mode’s impact on search advertising growth, plus ongoing diminishing returns from ad tech.

At DMBS, agency leaders explained why retail media offers new opportunities to harness closed-loop measurement in a way that benefits both upper- and lower-funnel marketing efforts.

Get access to tools and analysis to stay ahead of the trends transforming media and marketing

#7320. Old Navy flies with celebs, Gap keeps it acapella for holiday campaigns

The sister brands are sticking to their respective marketing strengths as the holiday season kicks into high gear.

Old Navy has launched its holiday campaign with a star-studded spot, per details shared with Marketing Dive. Actors Alan Cumming and Meg Stalter serve as flight attendant and pilot, respectively, on the “Jingle Jet,” where passengers include Paris Hilton, Charli D'Amelio, actor True Whitaker and influencer Vidya Gopalan and her family.

The campaign follows the recent launch of a holiday campaign from sister brand Gap that is centered around music. Taken together, these see the brands sticking to their respective marketing strengths as the holiday kicks into high gear.

Old Navy’s campaign was created with the brand’s in-house team and agency BBH USA. It was directed by duo Adam & Dave, with Academy Award-winner Autumn Durald Arkapaw as director of photography. The ad spotlights fashions starting at $7.99 including sequined dresses, matching pajamas and performance fleece and ends with the value-oriented tagline “More Merry For Your Money.”

“At Old Navy, we believe the holidays should be filled with more fun, more style and more possibilities for everyone. From festive fashion to gifts for the whole family, we’re making it easier to celebrate big without compromising,” said Zac Posen, chief creative officer for the brand in press materials.

The campaign is the first effort launched during the tenure of Old Navy CEO Michael Francis, who stepped into the role on Nov. 2, and the latest example of how the retailer has changed its marketing strategy after a recent sales slump.

“Our marketing has been totally rewired … to create more sharper price points and more value orientation with, of course, the Old Navy narrative that keeps our brands really relevant,” Gap Inc. CEO Richard Dickson said during a Goldman Sachs conference in September.

The Old Navy holiday effort sees the brand re-team with Hilton, who featured in a summer campaign that drove engagement but not traffic, Dickson said at the conference. The brand’s last holiday campaign was in 2024 and starred Jennifer Hudson with a cast of lesser-known characters.

Gap on Nov. 3 rolled out the latest iteration of its “Give Your Gift” campaign, highlighting individuality, creativity and community in an ad that brings together three choirs to sing Jackson 5 classic "I'll Be There.”

“The spirit of this campaign celebrates unity and togetherness. That’s the sentiment we aim to bring to our customers this season, offering new opportunities to engage with our brand and create memorable experiences that bring Gap’s gifting destination to life, both digitally and IRL,” Gap CMO Fabiola Torres said in a statement.

The campaign spans digital, social, video and owned channels and will be extended through immersive shopping experiences, including a multiday pop-up opening in Williamsburg, Brooklyn, this month.

Gap launched the “Give Your Gift” campaign in 2024 with an ad that featured an a cappella version of Janet Jackson’s “Together Again” and returned in 2025 with an ad centered on a cover of Miley Cyrus song “The Climb.”

Gap Inc. comparable sales were down 1% year over year in the second quarter of 2026, with Old Navy down 4% and dragging down overall results at a time when the company's flagship brand Gap saw comp sales increase 10%. The company will release its third quarter earnings on Nov. 19.
