#7297. Creators see promise in Instagram’s long-form push, but question whether exposure is enough

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Last week, Instagram said users would be able to post videos up to 90 minutes long, a move aimed at attracting long-form YouTube creators. But, aside from offering a handful of select creators between $50,000 and $500,000 to post long-form content through its new “Features” feature, it has offered no concrete monetization pathway for creators, saying it’s “too early to share a long-form monetization model.”

Early reactions are mixed: Without a clear ad revenue plan, creators may hesitate to make original content for Instagram, while others see promise in the platform’s discovery tools and new TV app.

Digiday spoke with seven creator economy experts about what the change could mean for creators, agents, and marketers.

Instagram is known for reach, but engagement on Reels can vary (marketing software platform Cropink reports Reels get 36% more reach than carousels and 125% more than single-photo posts, but engagement lags). And creators question how that engagement translates into income.

Digital strategist and creator Gabriella Zutrau said that Instagram has great discoverability compared to other platforms, which is helpful for creators looking to build awareness. However, she was “disgusted” that it appears, at least for now, that the platform is “offering discoverability as a form of payment.”

Several of the creator managers and creators Digiday spoke with expressed concerns about the lack of an ad revenue structure for long-form content — especially in comparison to what is widely considered the best platform for earning, YouTube. But it’s increasingly difficult to get monetized on YouTube because of the thresholds required, and it may be harder after its requirements change next year.

“Exposure as a form of payment and with the intention of cutting into YouTube’s market share and cutting into the amount of views people are getting on YouTube — it’s just gross,” Zutrau said. “Instagram has never valued creators.”

Jo Wong, chief revenue officer at agentic AI creator commerce platform POP.STORE, agreed.

“If you talk to most creators, they’ll tell you that that’s really where a lot of the brand deals come through… Instagram in general never really had a super strong monetization program for creators off the get,” said Wong. “Frankly, I think they thought they could get away with [not having a long-form monetization model].”

Samantha Walters, vp of talent experience at Shine Talent Group, said she was “surprised” the platform hadn’t offered a competitive or comparable monetization infrastructure. “That’s why YouTube is so successful, and why creators give so much of their time and effort to it,” she added.

Nick Jacklin, president of Shorthand Studios, was more optimistic, though he emphasized that creators should be paid equitably and transparently, and noted that if Instagram starts running ads on long-form content, it’ll break creator trust.

“I’m all in favor of advocating for a similar model [to YouTube] where Instagram makes $1 [per view], some share of that goes to the creator…but if Instagram can show with the creators that do [Features] work early on that you can drive awareness, I think the monetization will follow,” Jacklin said.

A representative from Instagram told Digiday the platform is “exploring ways for creators to earn from brands, fans and Instagram, including performance-based incentives.”

For Alexandra Mathieu, vp of creative and strategy at Open Influence, the competition Instagram’s long-form push could breed is good for talent. “Instagram putting money behind creator acquisition gives managers more leverage in conversations with YouTube,” she said. “It also opens the door to negotiating beyond fees, including exclusivity, distribution, production support, and creative control.”

With monetization up in the air, are creators and creator agencies planning to do a strategic pivot? It depends on the content genre, it seems.

Mustafa Aijaz, vp of media company SoaR, which works with a lot of gamers and streamers, plans to use it as just another distribution platform for now. “We’ll post our content there, why not? But I’m not making content for Instagram at this moment in time,” he said.

Zack Evans, vp of creative strategy at Shorthand Studios, thinks interview-style shows and scripted comedy could work really well on the platform — especially with the revamped Instagram TV app, which he said promotes scrolling and discoverability more than YouTube does.

“Comedy is inherently surprising, so I think with Reels and Features now, being able to just scroll and see a scripted thing playing out, you don’t yet know what it is, and you’re going to be intrigued,” he said. “It’s much more a pure comedy experience…I think that there will be a certain sector of creators who will actually rise to [this] occasion.”

The ability to pull viewers in with short-form content and funnel them to long-form while remaining on the platform could be a boon for any kind of video creator.

“The top of the funnel is Instagram. [We’re always] trying to get them and trying to use Reels, use Shorts, use any kind of way to grab attention for people, and then try to funnel them over to a longer-form piece of content,” said Evans. “It’s really hard, and these platforms obviously aren’t the best of friends, and so they make it difficult for you to do that.”

The discoverability of short-form and the shareability of content on Instagram could help creators build bigger audiences on the platform, which they can then shop to brands.

Both Wong and Mathieu suggested creators might use Instagram’s Features to recycle older content for reach.

“I’ve encouraged creators to actually use their older content and put it on there,” said Wong. “Most of the views on your YouTube come within the first month, for example, or three weeks, then put your older catalog on Features and test into it. See how your reach is doing. What does it mean for you? Are you able to then get better brand deals, or how does that change?”

She’s advising creators to do that before “catalyzing” original content, whereas Mathieu thinks creators should use Features to use “existing programming as a springboard for new, Instagram-native series such as creator-led shows, movie-length content, longer-running series and video.”

It’s clear that creators are still sussing out Instagram’s latest feature. In a survey sent to 50 of Shine Talent Group’s creators, 93 percent said the platform would have to change its revenue policies to entice them to post long-form content; 100 percent already post on the platform.

Ad tech’s winners and losers emerge as CTV growth masks open-web struggles.

The New York Times, People Inc., News Corp and Reuters report revenue growth despite AI disruption, but face tougher comparisons ahead.

DoorDash’s announced plans to acquire Instacart for $17 billion point to the constant need to gain market share by diversifying services and offerings.

#7296. Meta makes Muse agents speak

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The artificial intelligence-powered tool will be able to recognize conversation queries and respond in a voice selected by the user.

Meta is giving its Muse AI agents speaking capabilities in an effort to make them more engaging and easier to use.

Muse users will be able to select a voice for their personal artificial intelligence-powered agent and empower audio queries.

As explained by Meta: “Sometimes it’s easier to think out loud. With voice, you can talk through an idea with your Muse and let it take shape without worrying about the exact prompt.”

Meta said this should make it easier to use Muse to plan different projects, including work-related prep.

“Some things are better rehearsed out loud,” the company said. “Prepare for a job interview, run through a presentation, or talk through a pitch — hearing a response and answering in the moment makes it feel more like the real thing.”

Meta said the Muse avatar will also be able to express more as it speaks, and will use subtle expressions and gestures that match the tone of the conversation.

“It reacts while you talk, so you can tell it’s following along,” Meta said. “And you choose how present you want it to be: voice only, the avatar alongside your conversation, or full screen when you want to talk face to face.”

Users will even be able to give their Muse characters a custom voice, like giving it a New York accent or making it speak like a pirate.

Which, for some reason, Meta is convinced is something that people want, despite similar options falling flat with users previously.

Back in 2023, Meta introduced celebrity-styled AI chatbots on Messenger, which enabled users to engage with AI bots that could reply, in text form, in the style of various celebrities.

Nobody cared, and less than a year later, Meta shut this feature down due to lack of interest.

But Meta wasn’t done. In August 2024, Meta launched a limited audio version of the same, with the company paying millions of dollars to record the voices of actors such as Judi Dench, Keegan-Michael Key and Awkwafina for AI chatbots.

That didn’t resonate either. Meanwhile, The Wall Street Journal reported in April 2025 that some users had used the feature to generate explicit content voiced by these celebrities. That ultimately led to the demise of the feature before an expanded roll-out.

The addition of custom voices for Muse bots isn’t exactly the same, but even so, Meta seems very dedicated to a novelty idea that nobody else seems to care about.

Either way, users can now give their Muse agent a voice, so that they can walk around in public looking totally normal as they talk out loud to an AI character on their phone.

#7295. L’Oréal’s evolving microdrama strategy puts entertainment over product

A ReelShort series from Urban Decay themed around ghosting dates generated 3.8 million views in five days.

L’Oréal was early to the branded microdrama boom in the U.S., running a holiday effort for Maybelline last year that riffed on Hallmark romance movie tropes. The beauty and cosmetics giant around Halloween again dipped its toe into the short-form video genre that has ballooned in popularity as marketers produce entertainment they hope can attract scroll-oriented viewers but requires a different touch than a glossy ad campaign.

The company’s Urban Decay brand on Oct. 24 debuted a vertical video series on ReelShort titled “Ghost Hunter Hotline,” which runs six episodes that generally last just a few minutes each. The story follows protagonist Zoe as she dishes out justice to romantic interests who suddenly went silent on the communications front, or “ghosted” her and her friends, while showing off Urban Decay’s aptly named Ghosting Spray product. A former fling eventually returns and throws a wrench in some of Zoe’s plans, the type of soapy twist typical to cliffhanger-heavy microdramas. The overarching idea was to blend elements typical to the spooky season with tongue-in-cheek commentary on modern dating culture.

“Ghost Hunter Hotline” generated 3.8 million views in the five-day window after launch while sitting at No. 2 on ReelShort’s most-viewed shorts ranking, according to data shared by the brand. L’Oréal’s microdrama experiments are part of a larger effort to think differently from an old-school advertising mindset that puts the chief focus on product messaging and brand story.

“It’s something that we’ve now known in the creator era for the last couple of years, which is that you really need to think about your audience first, and you think about entertainment first as the primary purpose [of marketing],” said Han Wen, chief digital and marketing officer at L’Oréal USA.

Rather than create the microdrama content with an external agency, as it had done in the past, L’Oréal produced, developed and distributed “Ghost Hunter Hotline” in collaboration with ReelShort, relying on its knowledge of what appeals to the genre’s audience. ReelShort, which is owned by Crazy Maple Studio, is one of the leading dedicated microdrama platforms, on track to generate over $1 billion in revenue this year, according to third-party industry analyses.

“We went into it knowing that we wanted to test directly with a platform rather than a more hybrid format,” said Wen.

Urban Decay proved to be the right fit for the content type in a L’Oréal portfolio that spans dozens of brands. Halloween is a major occasion for the luxury label, which was founded in the mid-’90s in Los Angeles and promotes an edgier, antibland image. L’Oréal acquired Urban Decay in 2012 to reach higher-end young shoppers.

Other marketers are turning to microdramas because they can be turned around quickly and on the cheap. “Ghost Hunter Hotline” came together faster than a traditional ad campaign, in about four weeks, according to Wen.

“Speed is really important in the current context of marketing, where trends move very quickly,” said Wen.

Wen acknowledged that not all of L’Oréal’s bets on emerging social video spaces have panned out in the past. She pointed to Periscope, a now-defunct livestreaming service under Twitter, and BeReal, a pandemic-era flash in the pan. Still, Wen expressed optimism about microdramas, which have become a mainstream fixture in China and continue to see traction in the U.S., where mobile-first viewing is becoming more dominant.

“You realize that there is something here that people do engage with organically,” said Wen of microdramas.

“To me, it seems like it's worth learning in terms of how U.S. audiences — and then consumers of our brands — would adapt to that behavior here and make sure that we're on the forefront of that,” Wen added. “The only way that we know that is to actually engage with it and try a couple of different things.”

#7293. McDonald’s US CMO steps down as sales decline

As the fast-food giant searches for a new marketing leader, it will split the role between two longtime executives on an interim basis.

Alyssa Buetikofer, chief marketing and customer experience officer of McDonald’s USA, will leave the company next month to spend more time with her family, according to an internal note obtained by Marketing Dive. A 15-year veteran at McDonald’s, the executive was appointed to her latest role less than two years ago when Tariq Hassan stepped down.

As the company conducts a search for its new U.S. CMO, its marketing will be supported by two current executives in interim roles. Global CMO and Head of New Business Ventures Morgan Flatley will also serve as interim chief creative officer, while company veteran Charlie Newberger will serve as interim senior vice president, U.S. chief marketing and commercial officer.

The shakeup arrives a day after McDonald’s reported that U.S. comparable sales declined 0.2% in Q3 2026. However, global comparable sales increased 1.5% and the company beat Wall Street expectations.

Buetikofer will remain with McDonald’s through Dec. 18 to help support the leadership transition. The note cited her impact on McDonald’s business, where she recently oversaw the return of Snack Wraps and Monopoly, as well as the launch of a new beverage platform and a successful Grinch collaboration.

Flatley previously served as U.S. chief marketing and digital customer experience officer from 2017 to 2021 before taking on the global leadership role. She is described in the note as the driving force behind the brand’s “Fan Truths” strategy and the “Famous Orders” platform.

“In today’s environment, creativity is the difference maker for brands, and Morgan’s leadership puts us in a strong position to develop big ideas that build the brand and accelerate growth,” according to the note.

Newberger has been at McDonald’s for more than 17 years and was most recently corporate vice president and global category head for beverages and desserts. In the interim, he will lead marketing operations and work to translate strategy into commercial execution across the U.S. business.

The leadership moves and CMO search come as McDonald’s works to implement a strategic plan that it unveiled in June and detailed at a September investor day. Marketing is part of a consumer-focused pillar of the plan that includes creating brand-led content and co-creating with fans to reach consumers in relevant and personalized ways. McDonald’s last month also stepped away from the FIFA World Cup, a move that could disrupt the QSR sports sponsorship space.

#7291. Container Engineer at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth. More than 9 million developers and 250,000+ customers now build on Supabase, and the majority of new databases on the platform are created by AI app builders — so the systems underneath have to stay reliable while they grow fast.

Container Engineering (ConEng) owns the internal container platform that runs Supabase's multi-tenant, multi-region services — the managed Kubernetes platform that other engineering teams build and deploy on top of. We own cluster lifecycle and cluster classes, multi-cluster and multi-region topology, capacity, scheduling, isolation and runtime safety, the higher-level platform APIs, the Kubernetes deployment contract and Argo semantics, rollout primitives, and runtime configuration and secret injection.

Your job is to make the platform itself trustworthy, observable, and cheap to operate at scale — and to raise the reliability bar for everyone who depends on it. This is not a gatekeeper role. You'll make the reliable path the easy path: building the automation and platform APIs that let product teams run safely by default, instrumenting what we operate, and making sure that when something breaks we detect it and recover fast.

Tooling & ecosystem — deep knowledge of the container and orchestration landscape (Kubernetes, containerd/OCI, the CNCF ecosystem) and a clear point of view on how to compose it.

Software engineering — designing and building the platform's automation, controllers, and internal tooling as production code, including solutions that don't come pre-shaped by an existing tool.

Operational / SRE practice — the discipline (SLOs, error budgets, incident response, ORRs) that runs the platform at scale as a predictable one.

We expect you to be genuinely deep in at least one of these, and many of our strongest candidates are deep in two (usually leaning toward one). Bringing real strength across all three is rare and exceptional — and even then, no one is equally strong in all three at once, so you'd focus where you're most needed at any given time. Different profiles are welcome here; tell us which of these is your center of gravity.

Regardless of the skill set, all team members carry a share of the team's on-call rota, responding to platform incidents and driving them to resolution and follow-up.

Define SLIs/SLOs and error budgets for the container platform itself, and build error-budget policies that actually gate platform changes.

Own the Operational Readiness Review (ORR) process for new platform capabilities and major changes.

Design and track metrics for platform uptime, and increase observability into the platform by capturing the right metrics, logs, and traces across the platform layers.

Drive the incident-to-improvement pipeline: facilitate blameless postmortems, connect findings to readiness gaps, spot repeat failure patterns, and push through systemic fixes.

Build and maintain the platform's automation, controllers, operators, and internal tooling — writing production code and shipping it, not just wiring together existing tools.

Design and implement the platform-side CI/CD and rollout primitives that speed up platform deployments while working within proper change and release management.

Implement and maintain security automation for the platform: intrusion detection, automated remediation, and patch management.

Identify and quantify operational toil, and build the automation that removes it rather than just documenting it.

Contribute to the platform's continual testing automation — the test suites and pipelines that validate platform releases are fit for purpose before they reach tenants.

Act as a platform reliability and architecture expert — contribute to failure-mode analysis, dependency mapping, and resilience design for multi-tenant container workloads.

Evaluate and evolve the platform's tooling ecosystem (orchestration, runtime, networking, isolation, and observability stack) with a clear, opinionated view on trade-offs.

Improve system architecture to reduce infrastructure cost while balancing security, reliability, and performance across tenants.

Guide teams building on the platform to design sustainable on-call practices for their own services: alert quality, escalation paths, runbook coverage, and noise reduction.

Have 5+ years in platform engineering, container/Kubernetes platform operations, SRE, or production infrastructure, and have operated production systems at scale and carried on-call for them.

Are genuinely deep in at least one — ideally two — of the three strength areas below (and tell us which is your center of gravity):

Tooling & ecosystem — know the container/orchestration landscape well: Kubernetes, containerd and the OCI/CRI standards, and the surrounding CNCF ecosystem (e.g. Argo CD/Argo Rollouts for GitOps and progressive delivery; Cilium/eBPF networking and the Gateway API; Karpenter/KEDA-style autoscaling; and workload-isolation runtimes such as gVisor or Kata Containers). You reason about trade-offs rather than reaching for defaults.

Software engineering — write real production code (Go and/or a comparable systems language) and can build controllers, operators, and platform APIs — stepping back to the underlying problem and building the right solution when no off-the-shelf tool fits.

Operational / SRE practice — are fluent in SLOs, error budgets, and operational KPIs; have led incident response and blameless postmortems; and have driven down MTTD/MTTR for systems you operated.

Have worked with large-scale multi-tenant systems and understand isolation as a spectrum — from namespaces, RBAC, network policies, and resource quotas through to stronger primitives such as virtual clusters (e.g. vCluster) or VM-class runtimes.

Script and automate to eliminate toil rather than absorb it.

Experience with managed database platforms or Postgres.

Familiarity with OpenTelemetry, Prometheus/VictoriaMetrics, Grafana, or similar observability tooling.

#7285. American Express Picks Jess Ling as Its Next CMO

American Express has named its next chief marketing officer (CMO) in Jess Ling, who is currently its evp of global brand advertising.

She’ll take the seat Nov. 30, according to a letter to employees from Howard Grosfield, group president of U.S. consumer services, shared on the company’s website.

Ling succeeds Elizabeth Rutledge, who plans to retire in March after more than three decades with the financial institution.

Ling brings more than 20 years of experience in marketing, advertising, and media.

The exec previously led B2B marketing at Verizon Media. She joined American Express in 2019 and started in the global advertising and brand management team in 2023.

Mitchell Parton is ADWEEK's senior commerce reporter, covering retail media as well as the rise of agentic and social commerce. Before joining ADWEEK in 2026, he was a reporter at Digiday Media's Modern Retail and before that wrote for several regional publications in Texas.

#7275. How Athletes Are Becoming the Media Companies of Tomorrow

Women’s sports are gaining unprecedented momentum, but how can brands turn that growing attention into meaningful partnerships that deliver lasting cultural and commercial value?

It starts with authenticity, collaboration, and a deeper understanding of the communities they serve.

In this episode of Adspeak by ADWEEK, host LaTecia Johnson, founder and CEO of Ingenius, brings together three sports marketing leaders: Shayne Odum, director of global partnerships & media sales at the Phoenix Suns, and former director of corporate partnerships and sales at the Atlanta Dream; Naz Hillmon, forward with the Atlanta Dream in the WNBA; and Connor Thompson, senior manager of energy at Reebok.

Recorded live at Brandweek 2026, the conversation explores how athlete partnerships, brand collaborations, and the growing influence of women’s sports are reshaping the marketing landscape.

The discussion examines why athletes are increasingly becoming media companies in their own right, how brands can move beyond transactional endorsements to build authentic relationships, and why cultural relevance requires more than simply attaching a logo to a sporting event.

The speakers also explore the importance of community pillars in evaluating brand alignment, giving athletes greater control over their storytelling, and creating partnerships that reflect shared values.

Naz Hillmon is a professional basketball player for the Atlanta Dream in the WNBA, currently in her fourth year with the organization. Originally from Cleveland, Ohio, and a University of Michigan graduate, Hillmon has established herself as a dynamic athlete and emerging brand partner.

Shayne Odum is the former Director of Partnerships and Sales for the Atlanta Dream and currently the Director of Global Partnerships and Media Sales at the Phoenix Suns. Shane brings a background in sports marketing and community-focused brand strategy. With expertise in building meaningful partnerships that align with organizational values, Shane shares strategic approaches to evaluating brand collaborations through the lens of community pillars: growing the game of basketball, empowering girls and women, and equity for all.

Connor Thompson is Senior Manager of Energy at Reebok, overseeing brand campaigns, product collaborations, and cultural partnerships that drive brand perception and equity. With four years at the brand, Thompson articulates Reebok’s strategy of creating authentic, long-term relationships with athletes and creators rather than chasing trends.

LaTecia Johnson is CEO and Founder of Ingenius, a platform building social commerce programs that connect brands, athletes, and creators as the media companies of the future. Her expertise centers on mapping cultural trends and evaluating the relevance and ROI of brand partnerships in the evolving creator economy.

[05:14] The Three Pillars Behind Authentic Brand Partnerships — Strong athlete partnerships begin with shared values, not short-term visibility. Shayne explains how the Atlanta Dream evaluates potential partners against three community pillars: growing basketball, empowering girls and women, and equity for all. Brands must demonstrate a genuine connection to at least one of these pillars before a partnership moves forward. Examples like Georgia Power and Cash App show how this framework can turn brand collaborations into meaningful, multi-year relationships rooted in community impact.

[08:52] Give Athletes the Freedom to Tell Their Own Stories — Authentic athlete endorsements work best when athletes control their own narratives. Naz explains why she won’t associate her name with brands that don’t reflect her identity and why her content needs to feel personal rather than scripted. By sharing interests beyond basketball, including her passion for photography, she gives audiences a fuller picture of who she is. For marketers, the lesson is to treat athletes as creative partners, allowing brand storytelling to develop organically.

[11:30] Why Great Partnerships Begin Before the Contract — Meaningful brand collaborations start with personal relationships, not contract negotiations. Connor shares how Reebok invests time in getting to know athletes through experiences such as family dinners and archive tours before discussing commercial opportunities. This relationship-first approach helps brands understand an athlete’s values and vision before building campaigns or products around them. Reebok’s work with Angel Reese and Nate Ament demonstrates how investing in trust early can create more authentic, culturally relevant partnerships over time.

[16:50] Measure Partnerships by Their Return on Influence — Brand awareness alone isn’t enough to demonstrate the value of sports sponsorships. LaTecia explains why marketers should establish measurable KPIs before an activation takes place, using metrics such as earned media value to assess return on influence. Defining these objectives upfront gives teams a clearer picture of what their investment delivers and makes it easier to justify future spending. For marketers, the shift is from simply generating visibility to demonstrating measurable impact through athlete partnerships and brand collaborations.

[20:21] Real Brand Commitment Goes Beyond the Big Campaign — Successful partnerships are built through intentionality and consistent follow-through, not just impressive launches or one-off activations. Shayne explains why brands supporting women’s sports must demonstrate a sustained commitment through ongoing communication, repeat collaborations, and genuine community engagement. He points to Cash App’s debt-forgiveness initiative as an example of how trust can translate into meaningful impact. For marketers, the takeaway is that authentic investment and long-term relationship-building create more lasting brand equity than short-lived campaign visibility.

Select ADWEEK as one of your favorite sources and stay up to date on our latest content.

Buetikofer has been with the business for 15 years, taking the U.S. gig when Tariq Hassan left in 2025

SharkNinja's first global festive push unites Beckham, Tom Brady, Kevin Hart and Aryna Sabalenka as it looks to build a more unified brand identity

#7273. Chief Customer Officer at Xapo Bank

Diversity is at the heart of who we are at Xapo Bank. We’re a fully distributed team of over 160 Xapiens that work remotely from 50+ countries around the world.

Our beginning: A world that enjoys economic freedom and wealth protection,no matter where you live or who is running your country.To achieve that, we search the world for the best people for the job. We work hard, think globally, and inspire each other to learn and grow. We are committed to changing the way things are done.

Although we are headquartered in Gibraltar, this is a full time, 100% remote position Work from anywhere!

Xapo's product suite has grown from a Bitcoin vault into a full member proposition: BTC and USD accounts that pay interest, a global card with Bitcoin cashback, Bitcoin-backed loans, a growing suite of BTC wealth and credit products, and access to stocks, ETFs, and stablecoins, all inside one regulated relationship.

Xapo is a bank, and a bank earns on the money its members choose to hold with it. Each of those products shipped well. What hasn't yet existed is a single owner of the economics of the customer relationship: one person accountable for winning the right members, growing what they hold with us, and keeping them, and for the balance-sheet products that make that possible.

The Chief Customer Officer is a new role at the top of the house, reporting to the CEO. You will run the stream that grows what members hold with Xapo, and you will own the Customer P&L, retail and institutional. It brings growth, client coverage, product management, and product design under one leader who is judged on customer contribution, the assets attracted and kept, and the members who stay. This is a customer-focused role in the most commercial sense: what a member earns from us, not the feature we shipped, is the centre of every decision.

The measure of success is a Customer P&L that grows on what members hold and keep: LTV:CAC improving, approved-to-paid rising, churn falling, and customer contribution the business can plan against.

This is a banking role, not a requirements-gathering role, a marketing role, or a role you can run from a dashboard. Products are priced on the balance sheet and judged on the money they attract and keep.

This part matters as much as the mandate. The known failure mode for this role, in any company, is a CCO with a polished customer-insight deck and no ownership of the number. That is disqualifying here.

Day 30: The Customer P&L baseline is documented across retail and institutional: customer contribution, LTV:CAC, approved-to-paid, churn, net paid-member growth, and assets attracted and kept. You have formed your own independent view of where members are won, lost, and under-served, and shared it with the CEO and Board.Day 60: The unified Customer operating model is live: one set of key results across Growth, Client Coverage, Mission PMs, and Product Design, read weekly from one source. Every mission PM owns a product's contribution and its price, and the roadmap is ranked on what it earns, built with Finance and the line leads.Day 90: At least one measurable improvement in LTV:CAC, approved-to-paid, churn, or net new AUM flow is live and attributable to a change you made, not a feature ship, an outcome moved. Every cell in your line has defined key results and an owner who explains the number every week.

Shape the FutureImprove lives through cutting-edge technology, work 100% remotely from anywhere in the world.

Great work-life balanceBuild amazing things with a balance of autonomy and collaborative teamwork. Set your own work schedule and make use of a flexible PTO plan when you need to recharge.

Expect ExcellenceCollaborate, learn, and grow with a high-performance team. Learn how you learn best - from books to conferences, you’ll get a yearly budget for your individual learning and development goals.

At Xapo, we prioritize consumer protection and adhere to regulatory requirements by ensuring that all Xapiens are accountable for upholding principles of fair treatment, transparency, and ethical conduct in their interactions with customers and stakeholders.

#7236. Kenya Barris Bets on Black With Latest Revolt Reality Series

Known for his hit comedy series Black-ish and Grown-ish, Kenya Barris is making his way back into unscripted programming, joining Revolt to executive produce the sixth season of the Bet on Black reality series.

With Barris’ arrival, Bet on Black introduced a refreshed format when it premiered on Oct. 19, in which student founders from Historically Black Colleges and Universities (HBCUs) receive hands-on mentorship and share in a $500,000 growth fund, with the opportunity to grow their businesses while retaining 100% equity in their ventures.

This is Barris’ first project with Revolt since he was recently named vice chair of its creative studio, Revolt Labs. In addition, joining Bet on Black has given him the chance to reunite, for the first time since the conclusion of Black-ish, with actor Marsai Martin, who hosts the reality series.

Speaking to ADWEEK, Barris said he was attracted to the series because of the name, adding that the idea of promoting the next generation of entrepreneurs was amazing and that he was impressed by Revolt’s execution of the series, saying it was done “at a higher level than I even thought that it could be done.”

He said it was important to invest in the next generation of Black founders, especially those coming out of predominantly Black universities, so that they can be presented in a better light and not caught up in the sometimes negative and perverted assertions of Black culture.

Barris, who gained his reality TV experience working on America’s Next Top Model, was impressed by the founders’ ideas on Bet on Black, saying he believes their ingenuity and brilliance will better the world and make their big ideas sellable.

Backing all of this is Target, which has been part of Bet on Black’s success for all six seasons, since 2021. Target’s partnership with Revolt has directed about $2.69 million to Black founders and their businesses by providing access to capital, resources, and growth opportunities.

This season, the retail chain has invested $500,000 in the show’s growth fund. Beyond that, Target is supporting the show’s marketing through TV, digital, CTV, YouTube, social, on-site, and influencer channels.

Revolt CEO Detavio Samuels said that Target’s continued investment in Bet on Black signifies “the importance of building mission-driven and purpose-driven brands.”

He went on to say it’s not enough to build a great company that makes great products and generates profits: “You also have to help create the world that your audience wants to live in.”

Samuels says Revolt has been committed to uplifting the Black community and trying to solve some of its biggest problems, and Target’s partnership reflects an advertiser committed to doing that work with them.

Samuels regards Barris as exemplary among Black creators: “Beyond just a Black space, I would very easily say [Barris] is one of the most prolific minds out right now, creating.”

Making his time at Revolt even more rewarding for Barris is the chance to team up with Martin, who played Diane Johnson, the youngest daughter, on the hit ABC show.

Proud of how all the kids on Black-ish turned out, Barris singled out Martin, saying she was just “a spark of energy and a spark of light.” He said she has known how to handle herself, from the first day she stepped on the Black-ish set to now, when they reunited on Bet on Black.

“It was really special to me,” Barris said.

Openings: One; start as soon as practical

Super Organics is a portfolio of wellness and supplement brands based in Fort Lauderdale, Florida, including Super Speciosa, a kratom brand. We work with a globally distributed team and affiliated manufacturing and fulfillment operations, with a focus on quality, transparency, and reliable execution.

We are hiring a hands-on Supply Chain & Operations Manager to keep products in stock, manage purchasing and production commitments, and deliver measurable savings in packaging, inventory, and shipping. You will build practical planning tools and independently follow through with manufacturers, suppliers, and warehouse teams.

You will also coordinate packaging artwork, product launches, and brand-side quality workflows. This role requires clear communication, sound judgment, and the ability to turn sales and inventory data into timely decisions.

• Build and maintain SKU-level forecasts and replenishment plans using sales history, subscriptions, promotions, growth, and seasonality.

• Account for usable inventory, quality holds, raw-material specifications, open orders, testing time, production lead times, minimum orders, and safety stock.

• Identify stockout risks early, recommend actions, and balance availability against working capital and excess inventory.

• Create purchase orders and track quantities, delivery commitments, receipts, remaining balances, and invoice discrepancies.

Manufacturing and supplier accountability

• Serve as the brand's operational contact for affiliated manufacturing teams, external manufacturers, and packaging suppliers.

• Obtain clear commitments for production, quality release, pricing, quantities, and delivery dates; follow up and escalate risks promptly.

• Coordinate production priorities, new product launches, reformulations, and transitions with internal teams.

• Maintain a clear record of confirmed commitments, open issues, accountable owners, and next actions.

• Source and compare domestic and international packaging suppliers, including suppliers in Asia, based on total landed cost, quality, lead times, and minimum orders.

• Coordinate designers, printers, suppliers, and manufacturers to complete artwork and packaging on time.

• Maintain approved specifications, artwork versions, proofs, and production files; verify that each run uses the correct materials.

• Measure realized savings after freight, storage, inventory, and service tradeoffs.

• Own operational follow-through for brand-side quality and compliance, including documented product release, hold, and disposition decisions within approved procedures and decision authority.

• Apply familiarity with dietary supplement cGMP, including 21 CFR Part 111, to supplier documentation, product specifications, and outsourced manufacturing responsibilities.

• Coordinate testing and documentation review, label compliance reviews, and final artwork approval, involving qualified quality, regulatory, or legal expertise when needed.

• Maintain lot traceability, complaint records, and recall readiness; coordinate investigations, corrective actions, and customer-facing recall execution with the appropriate teams.

• Ensure manufacturers and warehouses act on the correct product status and escalate issues requiring specialist review.

• Coordinate receiving, inventory accuracy, fulfillment, and exceptions with warehouse and logistics teams, including affiliated operations and external providers.

• Reconcile purchase orders, shipment records, warehouse receipts, and invoices.

• Analyze parcel, freight, fulfillment, and packaging costs; evaluate carrier mix and service options without compromising delivery performance.

• Build useful spreadsheets, dashboards, and SOPs showing stock coverage, at-risk SKUs, purchasing needs, order status, and savings.

Supply risks are identified early, orders have reliable dates and clear owners, inventory records reconcile, packaging is ready for production, quality decisions are documented, and cost reductions are measurable. Leadership can see what needs attention without chasing routine updates.

#7218. The 2026 Political Ad Outlay Was the Dumbest Money Spent In the History of Advertising

2026 political advertising was a disgrace.

Campaigns torched billions on placements that had little-to-no impact on voter behaviors. Many ads backfired, and in a few cases — such as Texas and Ohio — did so spectacularly.

AdImpact says 2026 was the most expensive election year in history (including both presidential and midterms cycles), with $12 billion spent on ads. Most of that money was backed Republicans, but the GOP wipeout makes you wonder if it had any effect. Could things have gone worse for the party if campaigns and PACs had run no ads at all? Doubtful.

The most egregious examples are truly embarrassing for the losing campaigns. In Texas, Elon Musk’s over-the-top spending to back Trump loyalist Ken Paxton created an opening for James Talarico to surge on a “Texas is not for sale” message.

In Ohio, Vivek Ramaswamy’s desperate and gross ad featuring Amy Acton’s mother may go down as the worst miscalculation in political advertising history.

It wasn’t just Republicans who hurt their chances with terrible ad strategies. In Maine, Janet Mills had to bow out of the Dem primary after negative ads targeting Graham Platner backfired and her support dried up.

Beyond that, a whole bunch of deceptive and undisclosed AI-generated ads triggered negative media coverage and may have run afoul of the Federal Elections Committee.

It’s not that political ads never work, but they can’t accomplish what many campaigns seem to want them to do.

Specifically, ad spending will never overcome a broad-based shift in voter sentiment of the kind we just saw. Republicans outspent Democrats by $200 million in Texas, by $100 million in Ohio, by $84 million in Michigan. If such princely sums were spent to promote snacks or mobile games rather than political brands, the results would be real and highly measurable. Where’s the return on all this campaign spending? Nowhere to be found. Turns out, no amount of money could sway angry Texas voters to back Ken Paxton. When people have turned their back on a whole product category — whether MAGA-style politics or sugary drinks — the individual products and brands in that category stand no chance.

Also, 2026 campaigns vastly overrelied on a last minute onslaught of ads to change minds. Relentless October spending just dials up the saturation, inviting voter annoyance or outright hostility. The very best use of political advertising may be to build awareness for a candidate early in the cycle. Once that opportunity has been squandered, all that remains is to browbeat voters who already know (or think they know) your candidate. This is probably more true in a midterm race, when voters are on the whole more informed than in general elections.

Finally, please stay away from AI-generated creative — especially if it’s meant to deceive or confuse. The use of AI in political ads dramatically ramped up over the last two years, from $50,000 to at least $137 million. According to The Wesleyan Media Project, such ads are heavily skewed toward Republican campaigns. Fully 81% of AI-created ads supported GOP candidates and issues, compared with just 12% of Democrats. This makes some sense, since voters on the left generally have been louder in their disapproval of AI. But the public ire toward AI is increasingly bipartisan. The most telling thing about AI-generated ads is how many of them exclude disclosures — suggesting campaigns either know they’re unpopular, are deliberately misleading voters, or both.

No lie: The 2026 political ad outlay may have been the dumbest money spent in the history of advertising. Even so, media and tech platforms will be glad to take political money from the same undiscriminating sources again in two years’ time. It’s kind of a travesty.

The good news is, our relentless politics means campaigns will have the chance to mend their ways all too soon. I’d urge you to think like a smart commercial marketer: Find brands that are worth promoting, build the story early and learn from the mistakes of the past.

Zach Rodgers is a journalist, editor and podcast producer/host focused on the digital media and advertising industries.

Select ADWEEK as one of your favorite sources and stay up to date on our latest content.

Abdul El-Sayed, James Talarico, and Zohran Mamdani built followings on character as much as policy.

The merged company, WRLabs, pairs Rembrand’s AI with Whisper World’s decade of in-show ads across Asia, Africa, and the Middle East

#7210. Election Night 2026 Ratings: NBC News is the Most Watched Network

Steve Kornacki‘s data wizardry helped propel NBC News to the No. 1 spot in broadcast and cable in total viewers and the Adults 25-54 demo during Tuesday night’s coverage of the 2026 midterm elections.

NBC News’ performance comes as, according to Nielsen Media Research, a total of 31.2 million viewers across 13 networks tuned in between 8 and 11 p.m. ET to watch the blue wave sweep across the nation as the Democratic Party won both chambers of Congress. (All times Eastern) This year’s numbers were significantly better than the last midterm election, up +23% compared to 2022.

Hover or tap a point for the exact viewers, the programming window, and the number of networks.

During primetime, NBC News registered 6.74 million total viewers and 2.794 million demo viewers. Compared to 2022, NBC News was up by triple digits, 117% in total viewers and 164% in the demo.

In second place in both measured categories was ABC News, with 5.19 million total viewers and 1.672 million demo viewers. This represented gains of 57% in total viewers and 86% in the demo compared to the last midterm election cycle.

On the cable news front, Fox News led the way in total viewers, while CNN was the top network in the prized demo. Fox News had 4.315 million total viewers and 771,000 demo viewers. Compared to 2022, Fox News was down 42% in total viewers and 59% in the demo.

Meanwhile, CNN finished second among the cable networks with 4.147 million total viewers, but was No. 1 in the demo with 1.512 million viewers. Compared to 2022, CNN saw gains of 59% in total viewers and 43% in the demo.

#7168. Senior Product Builder - Hub at Camunda

Camunda is the enterprise platform for agentic orchestration, enabling organizations to coordinate AI agents, people, and systems across complex, end-to-end business processes. With built-in governance, auditability, and human oversight, Camunda gives enterprises the control they need to move AI from pilots to production — safely and at scale. Trusted by over 700 organizations worldwide, including 9 of top 10 US banks, Camunda helps enterprises boost operational efficiency, accelerate time-to-value, and deliver better customer experiences.Fully remote and global, we are in the middle of something bigger: transforming into an AI-first organisation, built on our own platform. We use Agentic AI to automate, orchestrate intelligent processes, and elevate human contribution across every team.Named GP Bullhound’s Top 100 Next Unicorn list, 2025 Great Place to Work certified. Visionary in 2025 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Business Orchestration and Automation Technologies. ranked 3rd in Flexa's 2026 Most Flexible Companies, We’re growing fast and looking for top talent to join our team. If you want meaningful work, visible impact and put something genuinely rare on your CV, keep reading.

Camunda Hub is where enterprises run Camunda at scale. From Hub they manage their clusters and tenants, decide who can do what, and roll changes out across the whole organization. We’re looking for a Senior Product Builder to own the backend and architecture that let Hub manage a whole fleet of clusters. Your first focus will be the mechanism that sends access, secret, tenant and licence changes out to every connected cluster and brings deployments, health and audit records back. From there, you’ll shape Hub’s foundations: its event model, the service boundaries and data model other teams build on, and how Hub behaves when things fail or get upgraded. The platform is your product. Your users are platform administrators, Centres of Excellence and the builders across Camunda who depend on what you create. You’ll join a new two-person pod in our remote-first team. You’ll have real ownership as the person who makes the call, and you’ll build AI-first, from written specs through to production. Our customers run mission-critical processes on Camunda, so your work will matter to them every day.

Take over Hub's fleet propagation mechanism from the team building its first version, and make it work for access, secrets, tenants, licences and custom roles

Write short design documents for foundational changes, get them reviewed by the teams that depend on them, and turn them into tested, shipped code

Design how Hub behaves when things go wrong: offline clusters, partial failures, Hub outages, and clusters being adopted or detached

Build the path from clusters back to Hub, starting with deployment inventory, health, audit records and licence status

Design Hub's first event and webhook mechanism, so admins and agents can react to changes instead of polling for them

Join the pod's rotations and Hub on-call, and help other builders take on backend work through reviews, guidelines and contract tests

Experience building and running backend systems in production that other teams or customers depend on

Comfort taking a problem nobody owns yet and turning it into a design, a plan and shipped code

Product judgment for technical users: you talk to the people who use your APIs and make trade-offs on their behalf

Clear, short writing that lets a remote team review and decide asynchronously

Care for how a system behaves when parts of it fail, not only for the happy path

Designed and shipped a control plane or similar system that pushes desired state to many targets. Knows outbox or event-log patterns, idempotent apply, versioning, retries and drift reconciliation

Senior backend engineering in Java (API design, relational data models, multi-tenant SaaS services) and hands-on Helm and Kubernetes for self-managed installations

Software architecture and failure analysis: partial failure, blast radius, consistency trade-offs. Has debugged production incidents in a distributed system and is ready for on-call

Has shaped an API or platform as a product, and writes design docs other teams can build on. Gets agreement across teams without formal authority

Builds AI-first from written specifications and checks AI output as carefully as any other code

Kubernetes operators or GitOps tools, for example Argo CD

OIDC, SCIM and permission models, or secret managers and licence systems

Camunda 8 or another workflow or orchestration engine

Enough frontend skill to build a thin UI on top of a backend capability

#7165. Mozaiq: Founding Account Executive

LiftPilot is an AI-powered website personalization platform for B2B companies. We help marketing and growth teams give every visitor a tailored website experience, turning more traffic into engagement, qualified pipeline and conversions.

The product is built, enterprise-grade and in the market. We’re live with our first pilots and the results so far have been exceptional.

LiftPilot is early enough that how we sell is still an open question, and you’d be the person answering it — figuring out who bites, what lands, which deals are worth chasing and which aren’t. You won’t be doing that from a standing start: the founding team is deep in this market, so the product knowledge, the context and the second opinions are all a message away.

You’d run the whole cycle yourself, target list through to signed. The pipeline you work is the pipeline you build, and what you learn building it is what LiftPilot’s sales motion becomes.

If you want a process to follow, this isn’t it. If you want to be the reason there’s a process at all, let’s talk.

Build your own target list and book every meeting through your own outreach: email, LinkedIn, phone and events.

Sell to VP-level and above marketing, demand gen and growth leaders at B2B companies.

Run discovery calls, and prepare a tailored demo for each first meeting.

Help your champion make the case internally, and position LiftPilot against existing tools, agencies and internal builds.

Negotiate and close new business, and set each new customer up for a smooth start.

Keep an accurate, honest pipeline in the CRM.

Bring what you hear from buyers back into the product.

MarTech sales experience.You’ve sold marketing technology, such as website personalization, ABM, CRO, advertising, analytics or content tools.

Early-stage experience.You've carried a number at an early-stage company, ideally as one of its first two salespeople.

Self-sourced pipeline.Most of your pipeline came from your own outreach, and you can walk us through how you built it.

Selling without proof.You've closed deals with no case studies or references to point to.

Strong writing.You write cold emails that get replies.

Organized and self-directed.You plan your week, follow up without being chased, and write down what's working.

Curiosity about the product.You want to understand how it works, not just pitch it.

You Close.You push deals to a decision rather than letting them drift. You’re comfortable asking for the signature, chasing the fourth follow-up, and naming the thing that’s blocking a deal instead of waiting it out.

Experience selling website personalization, ABM, CRO or experimentation platforms.

Experience navigating buying committees in mid-market and enterprise deals.

You need an SDR, a territory or an account list to hit your number.

Most of your past pipeline came from inbound or leads you were handed.

You're looking for a VP or Head of Sales title right away.

#7157. Senior Data Scientist, Places at Raya

In a world where many social apps measure their success by time spent online, Places offers a different kind of product built by Raya, a technology company focused on providing utility. Our apps, Raya and Places, are tools for discovery of and access to exciting people and places globally. We believe that by marrying great software with a membership-based community built around core values of trust, curation, and authenticity, we can provide solutions that offer something different to infinite content and AI-slop. Places’ mission is to help discerning global travelers find their favorite places wherever they go through expert curation and community trust.

As a Data Scientist on the Places by Raya team you will play a key role that will shape how the Places app uses data to drive business decisions. This hands-on role combines data modeling and reporting with product analysis and experimentation. While the product itself has existed since 2023 the culture is very much like a new start-up with leadership being heavily invested in this product space and this role being the first dedicated Data Scientist to this space and supported by a dedicated Data Engineer.

We are seeking an entrepreneurial and driven data scientist to identify opportunities to improve retention and help the team evaluate and act on them. This will be a fun role for someone who wants to build data solutions from the ground up and help shape how the entire team uses insights. This person should be able to articulate best practices, develop new analytical frameworks that tie user actions to outcomes, and strike the right balance between analytical rigor and pragmatic business action.

Successful candidates will demonstrate technical skills, product expertise, and business acumen, and be enthusiastic about making a positive impact through timely execution. You are passionate about leveraging the power of data to drive product changes with quality and agility and can demonstrate sound judgment under uncertainty.
